*Central Emblem Description: This page features a decorative paper insert with a colored circular emblem. The border of the emblem contains the title "HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY" <note>original Latin: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA"</note>. The center shows a "pelican in its piety," a traditional motif where a mother bird pecks her own breast to nourish her young with blood. Below this is a shield displaying four red roses on a white background, split by a cross—a classic Rosicrucian symbol. Above the scene, a radiant sun shines from a blue sky.*

<note>The term "Hermetic" refers to the secret teachings of Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy. The pelican symbolizes the stage of the alchemical process where the "Philosopher's Stone" is perfected through a process of self-sacrifice or internal circulation.</note>

# Contents
->of the subject matter contained in this polemical treatise on the Golden Fleece.<-

## First Section.

Following the introduction regarding the history of the Golden Fleece <note>The "Golden Fleece" is a famous mythological object that alchemists frequently used as a metaphor for the Philosopher's Stone or the "Great Work" of chemical transformation.</note>, this section contains some general matters as well as the origins and constituents of metals, including how they are converted into mercury.

§. 1. What is to be observed when providing a proof of the possibility of the <term>transmutation: the alchemical process of changing one substance into another, specifically base metals like lead into noble metals like gold</term> of metals?
§. 2. What is understood by the art of the transmutation of metals?

<summary>This table of contents page introduces a treatise on alchemy, specifically outlining the early chapters regarding the history of the Golden Fleece and the theoretical foundations of metallic transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Fleece, alchemy, transmutation, mercury, quicksilver, metal constituents, polemical treatise</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents from the previous page, detailing sections 3 through 14 of the treatise. The catchword at the bottom, "§. 15," indicates that the list of contents continues on the following page.</meta>

## Contents.

§. 3. Proofs can be conducted in three different ways.

§. 4. The difference between Mathematical and Philosophical proofs. <note>In the 18th century, "philosophical" proofs referred to those based on natural philosophy or experimental science—specifically alchemy in this context—while "mathematical" proofs relied on logical or geometric certainty.</note>

§. 5. A description of metal.

§. 6. Only two metallic origins according to the opinion of the Ancients. <note>The "Ancients" typically refers to classical Greek or medieval authorities who often believed metals were composed primarily of sulfur and mercury.</note>

§. 7. The three origins of the same according to the opinion of the Moderns. <note>This refers to the "Tria Prima" (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) popularized by Paracelsus and his followers.</note>

§. 8. By these origins, one does not mean common salt, sulfur, and quicksilver. <note>Alchemists were careful to distinguish between the common materials found in a shop and the "philosophical" or "sophic" versions of these elements used in their Great Work.</note>

§. 9. Why the philosophers give their origins these names.

§. 10. The three earths of Becher explained. <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682) was an influential chemist who argued that all matter was composed of three types of earth: the "stony," the "mercurial," and the "fatty" (or combustible) earth.</note>

§. 11. Proof that these earths are present.

§. 12. How the generation of metals occurs within the earth, what their sulfur is, and why Kunckel denies it? <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703) was a famous German chemist and glassmaker known for his skepticism toward certain mystical alchemical theories, despite being an alchemist himself.</note>

§. 13. Through common <term>dissolution: the process of breaking a substance down into its component parts using a solvent</term>, the origins of metals are not revealed; and that they share one essence with each other.

§. 14. The difference among metals is accidental, and what is understood by physical gold? <note>The term "accidental" here is used in the Aristotelian sense, meaning a property that does not change the essential nature of a thing. Alchemists believed all metals were essentially the same at their core, differing only in "accidents" like color or impurities.</note>

<summary>This page lists the table of contents for sections 3 through 14, focusing on the theoretical composition of metals, the "three earths" theory of Becher, and the chemical debates between modern and ancient authorities.</summary>
<keywords>Becher, Kunckel, Tria Prima, metallic origins, sulfur, salt, mercury, accidental properties, dissolution, physical gold</keywords>

# Contents.

§. 15. Whether metals can be reduced back into their <term>First Matter</term> <note>The "Prima Materia" or the original, formless substance from which all elements were thought to originate.</note>?

§. 16. The generation and transmutation of metals can occur in three different ways.

§. 17. That metals can be turned into mercury; this very fact shows the path to their transmutation.

§. 18. Concerning the preparation of the <term>Mercury of the Bodies</term> <note>A specialized alchemical term referring to the metallic essence extracted from solid metals like gold or silver.</note> through oil of vitriol <note>Sulfuric acid.</note>, and concerning its power to gild silver.

§. 19. Perpetual ore-growths <note>The belief that minerals "grow" like plants within the earth.</note>. The difficulty, and the fundamental principle, of creating the Mercury of the Bodies.

§. 20. A eulogy given to mercury by Kunckel <note>Johannes Kunckel (c. 1630–1703), a famous German chemist and glassmaker.</note>, which has been confirmed by experience; also its use in the art of <term>particular</term> <note>A "particular" transmutation refers to changing a specific amount of metal into gold, rather than possessing the universal Philosopher's Stone.</note> gold-making.

§. 13. A special experiment on the stabilization <note>original: "Beständigmachung" – the process of "fixing" a volatile substance so it withstands heat</note> of cinnabar <term>cinnabar: a bright red mineral (mercury sulfide) used as a primary source of mercury</term> into gold and silver.

§. 14. On the utility and use of hydrostatics <note>the study of fluids at rest</note> in proving the transmutation of metals.

§. 15. On the extraction of the souls, or the sulfur of metals, according to the teachings of Polemann and Helmont. <note>Joachim Polemann and Jan Baptist van Helmont were influential 17th-century chemists/alchemists</note>

§. 16. Espagnet’s dialogue, which denies the extraction of the soul, or the tincture of gold. <note>Jean d'Espagnet was a French jurist and alchemist</note>

§. 17. Answer to Espagnet’s doubts. Concerning extractive solvents <note>original: "Auflöß-Mittel"</note>, and a story of an English nobleman who, according to Boyle’s report, became wealthy through this particular <term>particular: an alchemical process that transforms only a portion of a metal, rather than a universal transformation</term>.

§. 18. Zwelfer’s testimony regarding the sulfur of gold, etc., and its increase according to Beccher’s teaching. <note>Matthias Zwelfer and Johann Joachim Beccher were prominent figures in early pharmaceutical chemistry</note>

§. 19. Beccher’s proof that the extraction of the tincture of gold is possible.

§. 20. Boyle’s experiment on the transmutation of gold into silver. <note>Robert Boyle, a founding father of modern chemistry, who remained interested in alchemical possibilities</note>

## Section Three

->On the artificial production of iron and sulfur, on the growth of metals, Homberg’s and other experiments, proofs for the possibility of metals, along with an answer to doubts.<-

§. 1. Beccher’s <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous alchemist and physician who influenced the phlogiston theory.</note> experiment on the production of iron using linseed oil and potter's clay <note>original: "Letten"</note>.

§. 2. Regarding the reasons for this experiment, which have been called into doubt, and the fact that iron exists throughout the entire world.

§. 3. On the utility of this experiment.

§. 4. On the transformation of iron into steel, and how the latter may be made soft.

§. 5. The artificial preparation of sulfur according to Stahl’s <note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), the chemist who developed the phlogiston theory.</note> doctrine.

§. 6. These same experiments performed by Mr. Geoffroy <note>Étienne François Geoffroy (1672–1731), a French chemist known for his "affinity tables."</note> with a wonderful stabilization of the spirit of wine <note>term: "spirit of wine" (distilled ethanol)</note>.

§. 7. Refutation of several opposing arguments, and on the utility of sulfur in the art of making gold.

§. 8. On the artificial growth of metals. On the first method through amalgamation, and the beautiful appearance of the golden rose. <note>The "golden rose" refers to a specific crystalline structure formed during the cooling of precious metal alloys.</note>

§. 9. On the second method of the growth of metals, using nitric acid <note>original: "Scheidwasser"</note>, or concerning the Tree of Diana <note>An arborescent growth of silver crystals from a solution, named for the Roman goddess associated with silver.</note>, as well as with sulfur.

§. 10. On the third method of this growth within the moisture of flint stones.

§. 11. On the life of metals, and whether flint is the matter of the Philosopher's Stone <term>Philosopher's Stone: a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</term> and the necessary mother of metals.

§. 12. On the chemistry of the King of France with Homberg <note>Refers to Wilhelm Homberg (1652–1715), a famous chemist who worked under the patronage of the Duke of Orléans and Louis XIV.</note>, and his experiments in general.

§. 16. On the rebirth and transformation of salts, and Glauber’s Alkahest <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber’s "Alkahest" was a hypothesized universal solvent capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid form.</note>.

# C o n t e n t s.

§. 17. On the correspondence between the petrifaction <note>The process of turning into stone</note> of wood and the transmutation of metals, and the astonishingly important masterpiece <note>original: "Kunststück"</note> of John the Evangelist.

§. 18. An experiment with horn-silver <term>Chlorargyrite; a silver chloride ore</term> and tin, or calcined ore, to produce gold.

§. 19. A similar experiment with iron and other things that impregnate silver, so that it truly acquires gold within itself.

§. 20. An experiment taken from elsewhere and derived from the realm of meteors, through which the truth of the golden art <note>Alchemical gold-making</note> is easily proven.

§. 21. A fable of the adultery of Mars with Venus, the wife of Vulcan, and the Hermaphrodite produced thereby, and of Diana acting through him so that she might obtain the radiance of Phoebus <note>In alchemical allegory, these planetary names represent metals: Mars (iron), Venus (copper), Vulcan (fire), Diana (silver), and Phoebus/Apollo (gold)</note>.

§. 22. Concerning proven testimonies and histories which confirm the transmutation of metals.

§. 23. An answer to the doubts of Mr. Friedrich Hoffmann <note>Friedrich Hoffmann (1660–1742) was a famous German physician and chemist who was often skeptical of certain alchemical claims</note>, which were raised against the possibility of this art.

## Fourth Section.

### Regarding several warnings which are useful in the art of making gold, especially via the <term>Particular Way</term> <note>The "Particular Way" refers to alchemical processes intended to transmute specific amounts of base metals into gold or silver, as opposed to the "Universal Way" of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

§. 1. Following the connection with the previous section, it is noted that all manner of errors occur within chemical labors.

§. 2. These are innumerable; yet from a few examples one may learn how difficult the journey to <term>Colchis</term> <note>A reference to the destination of Jason and the Argonauts in search of the Golden Fleece; a common alchemical metaphor for the difficult quest for gold.</note> truly is.

§. 3. Why the author had reservations about allowing this section to be released to the public.

§. 4. Not every black lime <note>original: "Kalk"; likely referring to a calx or precipitate</note> that settles in the <term>parting water</term> <note>Nitric acid, used to separate gold from silver.</note> is gold.

§. 5. An example of a silver which, being impregnated by <term>Mars</term> <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note>, was dissolved with parting water and caused iron to precipitate.

<summary>This table of contents page outlines the fourth section of the manuscript, focusing on practical warnings for alchemists and debunking false signs of successful gold production.</summary>

# C o n t e n t s.

§. 6. Other examples involving mercury and silver, as well as silver that has been cemented <note>Cementation is a dry alchemy process where a metal is heated with powdered reagents to purify it or change its properties.</note> with sulfurous substances.

§. 7. Similar examples cited by Becher <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a famous German chemist and physician known for his theory of "terra pinguis," the precursor to phallogiston.</note> regarding the "sand-growth" <note>original: "Minera arenaria"; likely referring to gold or silver found in sandy deposits or riverbeds.</note>.

§. 8. What constitutes "confirmation" <note>In this context, "confirmation" refers to the chemical stabilization or fixing of a metal so it does not lose its properties when heated.</note> according to Becher’s view?

§. 9. Concerning the unstable gold and silver described by Mr. von Suchten <note>Alexander von Suchten (c. 1520–1575), an alchemist and physician who wrote extensively on the secrets of antimony.</note>.

§. 10. The remedy against instability: turning the substance into glass and then driving it back <note>A process of vitrification followed by reduction back into a metallic state.</note>.

§. 11. The preparation of the mercury of antimony <note>original: "Merkur des Spiesglases"; antimony was frequently called "Spiesglas" in early modern German chemistry.</note> according to the teachings of Stahl <note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), the chemist who developed the phlogiston theory.</note>.

§. 12. Concerning silver-calx <note>A "calx" is the powdery residue left after a metal has been roasted or oxidized.</note>, which is white and volatile upon precipitation, and contains gold within it.

§. 13. How impure calxes attain a pure golden nature?

§. 14. Concerning the Melandinian Art <note>Likely a reference to a specific alchemical tradition or a localized metallurgical technique.</note>, or how to raise Rhenish gold to the grade of Hungarian gold <note>Hungarian gold was historically prized for its high purity compared to other European sources.</note>.

§. 15. Concerning the enhancement of gold.

# Contents.

§. 30. Regarding the erroneous opinion that lead and tin can be transformed into mercury. <note>The author is debunking the common alchemical belief that base metals could be "retrograded" into quicksilver.</note>

§. 31. Regarding the suspicious or presumed transformations into mercury.

§. 32. Regarding the tinctures <term>Tincture: a concentrated medicinal or alchemical substance intended to transform metals</term> which do not penetrate, and regarding the flux <term>Flux: a substance used to promote melting and fusion</term> that introduces everything.

§. 33. How the tinctures are fermented and given a physical body.

§. 34. How the unnatural and adverse essence of the colored metals is to be removed.

## Section One.

### Introduction.

In order, above all else, to provide the reason for the title of the "Golden Fleece," I consider that the story—which is spread everywhere—is hidden from no one; or rather the fable, wherein Phryxus <note>In Greek mythology, the son of King Athamas who fled his stepmother's wrath.</note>, a Theban, by the decree of fate, is said to have swum across the sea upon a ram whose wool shone like gold. He arrived in Colchis <note>original: "Colchus"</note>, a region in Asia which today is called Mingrelia <note>original: "Mikrelia"; a region in western Georgia.</note>, and there is said to have sacrificed the ram in honor of Jupiter and Mars <note>The Roman names for the Greek gods Zeus and Ares.</note>, placed it among the stars, and hung the golden pelt in the temple.

# Of the Golden Fleece

After this rare Fleece—by which others understand a book written on the science of the golden art <note>The "golden art" refers to Alchemy, the practice of transmuting base metals into gold</note>—very many are said to have striven in vain, because the gates of the temple were guarded by fire-breathing dragons, which Martial <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis, a Roman poet</note> calls oxen with fiery feet.

Jason, however, a son of the King in Thessaly, who was a quite particular lover of magic, is said to have traveled to Colchis to obtain this Fleece during the time of Gideon, around the year of the world 2727. He traveled on a ship called the *Argo* with more than fifty companions (among whom Hercules, Castor, Pollux, Admetus, Theseus, and Orpheus are named as distinguished artists <note>In this context, "artists" likely refers to those skilled in the hermetic or liberal arts</note>). When he arrived there, Aeëtes, the king of that region and master of the temple, refused him the Golden Fleece. Thereupon, with the help of Medea, the king's daughter—who loved Jason dearly and taught him the art of putting the fire-breathing dragons to sleep—he carried away both the Fleece and Medea from the barbarians and brought them to his fatherland.

The fable now turns (so as not to detain us long with it) to the idea that Jason [obtained] from the Colchians, a race of the Egyptian Sages...

## Section One.

...learned such secret arts, among which the artistic treatment of metals and minerals, so that they might be of use to mankind, was the most brilliant and excellent.

For as Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46 – 120 AD) was a Greek philosopher and biographer whose works were often cited by alchemists to provide historical authority to their claims.</note> says, there were various gold veins and streams in Colchis; and the Colchians are said to have collected the gold dust, which was scattered here and there upon the waters, using sheepskins. Once these were completely dried, shining grains of gold remained hanging in the wool.

And with this, we can explain the name of the "Golden Fleece" without any difficulty. <note>The author provides a rationalist or "euhemerist" explanation for the myth of Jason and the Argonauts, suggesting the Golden Fleece was actually a tool for gold mining.</note>

The fire-breathing dragons, however, sufficiently indicate the difficulty of working with fire and of conquering the raw matter.

And that one gives this the correct name for that very reason, you will come to realize when you follow the teaching of Philaletha <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes was the pseudonym of a famous 17th-century alchemist, likely the American George Starkey.</note> in the seventh chapter of the *Entry into the Closed Palace of the King* <note>original: "Introitus ad occlusum Regis Palatium," a foundational alchemical text first published in 1667.</note>: take four parts of the fiery dragon, which keeps the magical steel hidden in its belly, and nine parts of our magnet; mix them by means of the hot Vulcan <note>In alchemical shorthand, "Vulcan" refers to the fire of the furnace or the god of the forge.</note> and so forth.

# Of the Golden Fleece

This danger and difficulty cannot be overcome, and access will be granted to no one by the supreme commander, unless it is brought about through the help of the lineage of the unique Medea—that is, the mistress of wisdom and the arts. <note>Medea is interpreted here not as a sorceress, but as a personification of Sapientia or Wisdom, necessary for the alchemical or intellectual "quest."</note>

We believe that in this manner the entire meaning of the fable is made very distinct and clear.

Furthermore, we do not doubt that the most fortunate Duke of Burgundy, Philip the Good <note>original: "Philippus Bonus"</note>, who rightly bears this name, had well considered this explanation when he established that noble Order of the Golden Fleece; and indeed at the very time when many magnificent inventions, and especially the art of book-printing, became known in Germany.

And this is proven alone by the golden chain which the knights of this brilliant Order wear, the links of which represent a fire-steel <note>A "fire-steel" or strike-a-light was a metal tool used with flint to create sparks.</note> with stones or flints from which the flame glimmers, with the motto: **The blow comes first, then the flame** <note>original Latin: "ante ferit, quam flamma micat"</note>. At the bottom of the chain hangs the fleece of a lamb, or that Golden Fleece, with the inscription: **A reward for labors that is not small** <note>original Latin: "Pretium non vile laborum." This suggests that the spiritual or material rewards of the "Great Work" are of immense value.</note>

# Section One.

By which motto we, animated with philosophical diligence, hope it shall not be in vain or useless that we may strike some fire through an academic disputation <note>original: "Akademische Streit-Schrift"; a formal scholarly debate or thesis common in 17th and 18th-century universities</note> which might illuminate for us the Golden Fleece—that is, the matter, or the proof of the possibility of the transformation and improvement of metals. May Eternal Wisdom grant success to it!

->§. I.<-

Whoever wishes to prove the possibility of the transformation of metals must, above all things, cut off and prevent the doctrines and useless quarrels which are commonly conducted with great vehemence over all manner of assumed words.

And this shall be done if—after the manner of mathematicians, who premise their proofs with thorough descriptions—it is explained right at the beginning what is to be understood here under the name of "transformation," and specifically that of "metal"?

We shall pass over what can be disputed from the perspective of metaphysics regarding possibility. The thorough and complete description of the metals known to everyone will soon follow in §. 5.

### §. 2.

We say, therefore, that under the art of the **transmutation of metals**, we understand all those arts (however they may occur) which teach how to transmute and change metals, minerals, and all metallic stones in such a way that one can recognize—through the common and universally accepted tests—that they have become different, either entirely or in part, even in small measures, becoming either nobler or baser. For example: if someone produces and brings forth some silver from lead, or copper from iron, or vice versa, regardless of the method by which it happens. Such work, through which something of this nature is performed, may be called: purifying, refining, extracting, introducing, ripening, stabilizing, animating, increasing, uniting, taking away, adding, transposing, reversing, melting together, softening, thinning, thickening, bringing to the center <note>original: "concentratio"; the alchemical process of concentrating the essence or "soul" of a substance</note>, making alive, turning into mercury, driving together, driving apart, multiplying, equalizing, elevating, bringing into fermentation, cementing <note>a dry heating process used to refine gold or change the properties of a metal</note>, casting, separating, making into a tincture <note>the "philosopher's stone" in liquid or powder form used to transform base metals</note>, or under whatever name it may appear. And this may occur in a long or short time, either with one or several operations, with none, or one, or many

## Section One.

By means, with—or without fire, by the wet, or dry path. Let it be brought about in whatever manner it will. One may say of that which is produced, that it was either of the second effect, or only hidden according to its potential within another, or occurred through the displacement of parts, or accidental things; or it may be the transformation and change of the essential form, happened, composed, and brought to pass either through a penetrating spirit or essence, through a particular or universal tincture <note>A "tincture" in alchemy is a substance—often the Philosopher's Stone—capable of transmuting base metals into gold or silver</note>, through a higher and heavenly, or even through a divine power.

->§. 3.<-

In order that we may now prove the possibility of the transformation of metals thus described and defined, we have two paths toward it. The one is the path of generation <note>The natural growth or "birthing" of metals within the earth</note>, the other of dissolution, or that which, as is commonly said, reveals itself with proofs taken *a priori* <note>original: "à priori"; reasoning from theoretical deduction rather than observation</note>, or *a posteriori* <note>original: "à posteriori"; reasoning from observed facts or effects back to causes</note>. To these we may also well add the path of proven testimonies.

->§. 4.<-

It will not be unserviceable to note regarding proof of this kind, that it is among those

# Of the Golden Fleece

...is accepted by the <term>philosophers</term> <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to natural scientists and alchemists of the early modern period.</note> in a twofold manner. The first is when the proposition, or that which is presented, is proven to the opponent with such great rigor that a confession is forced from him; he is thus convinced, without any exception, of the certainty and truth of what has been said.

Of this kind are such proofs which take place almost exclusively in actual <term>mathematics</term> <note>original: "Mathesis"</note>. The second kind is that which we employ in most philosophical matters, being satisfied when we see that what has been said accords with sound reason, or at least does not contradict it; and when we furthermore perceive, through proper connection, how one thing flows from another. Of such a nature are approximately most generally accepted judgments and opinions of men, especially those which have reached the highest degree of credibility.

We shall therefore also be permitted to make use of this second kind of proof, provided that we are unable to make use of the first.

->§. 5.<-

We shall, however, rightly begin with the description of the metal.

## First Section.

We have previously described it in our handbook (Compendium) as a body composed of the viscous particles of <term>Mercurial Water</term><note>In alchemy, this refers to the "philosophical mercury," a fluid substance believed to be the base of all metals.</note>, which are brought together with salty and sulfurous, earthy, pure, and constant particles. Through the help of motion and subterranean heat, these are formed into a firm, hard, heavy substance or mass that can be drawn out, hammered, and melted in fire. It possesses varying weights and colors and is mixed within the earth among many stony, earthy, sulfurous, and other mineral materials, and is rarely found as its own pure body.

->§. 6.<-

What is to be made of this description will be further revealed in the following sections. Meanwhile, it is clear enough from this definition that metal is distinct from all other things.

Everyone also knows that among the ancient philosophers and in the school of the <term>Peripatetics</term><note>Followers of Aristotle's philosophy, who dominated medieval and early modern science.</note>, the internal beginnings of metals—form and matter—are called Sulfur and Mercury.

Furthermore, this opinion has been generally accepted: that all metallic bodies are composed of these

...two, purer or impurer, grown together with differing weight, cooking <note>The term "cooking" refers to the alchemical belief that metals "ripened" or matured in the earth through natural heat</note>, and according to the nature of the place and the soils.

->§. 7.<-

Just as the more recent Christians, who primarily know the doctrines of Paul, have maintained that not only man, but also all other things are composed of body, soul, and spirit, so too have Basilius Valentinus <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, though his writings likely date to the late 16th century</note> and Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note>, and others besides, applied this teaching mainly to the mixed composition of metals. And they have taught and described the closer origins and components of the same as **Salt, Sulfur**, and **Mercury** <note>original: "Salz, Schwefel und Queksilber"</note>—taken not indeed in the common, but in the philosophical sense—assigning a certain intermediary to those two extremes or things standing far apart.

->§. 8.<-

# Section One.

...that common flammable mineral, which is composed of an acid and a fatty substance, or the natural, dripping, fatty, and as it were resinous juice—or that juice made from flints and other minerals—which must be distilled or raised with a strong fire, and is yellow; <note>The author is likely describing sulfur, which in alchemy represents the principle of combustibility.</note> nor by "salt" did they wish to be understood common table salt, which is boiled from spring or sea water, or the hard salt which is dug from the Polish mines under the name of stone or rock salt, or hewn out of the Catalonian and other pits; nor saltpeter, alum, vitriol, and so forth, under the name of the "principles," whether universal or metallic.

Most of the Sages have issued this reminder, and we also have clearly taught it in the above-mentioned Handbook <note>original: "Compendio"</note>.

->§. 9.<-

For these common mixed things have only given the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists or natural scientists.</note> the opportunity to define and express their principles—which in themselves do not strike the senses—in such a way because a considerable portion of that very principle and constituent, whose name it bears, is found within each of them.

<meta>Continuity from page 11: The author continues the discussion on the three primary principles of matter, transitioning from theoretical definitions to the practical terminology used by alchemists.</meta>

# Of the Golden Fleece

This element, which imparts firmness, stability, or physical substance to every mixed thing, they have called **Salt**. This is primarily because even the invisible spirit of the universe, when it assumes a body and becomes perceptible to the senses, appears in no other form than that of salt.

And because **Sulphur** is flammable and oily, providing color and warmth—acting, as it were, as a balsamic soul—they did not hesitate to assign its name to this essential form and tincture <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" refers to a potent substance capable of infusing other matter with its qualities, often associated with the soul of the metal.</note>.

Likewise, they observed that common **Mercury** is fluid and entirely evaporates in fire. They believed, therefore, that it suited its volatile nature very well to be called "Spirit," or a highly volatile, equally essential, moist being. They have even called it the "radical moisture" <note>original: "Grundfeuchtigkeit"; a term for the vital fluid that sustains life and flexibility in matter</note>, just as they called the sulfurous component the "innate and implanted heat" created by nature.

->§. 10.<-

However, so that this philosophical and somewhat abstract principle of the **Three Beginnings** <note>The *Tria Prima*: Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury</note> might be more easily understood by chemists and metal-workers, the ingenious **Becher of Speyer** <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a famous German physician and alchemist known for his theory of three earths, which preceded the phlogiston theory.</note> did very well to devise three earths and

# Section One.

given, which correspond excellently with salt, sulfur, and mercury, as well as the three elements in which, according to the teachings of Aristotle <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE) proposed that all terrestrial matter was composed of four elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>, the animals dwell: namely earth, air, and water.

Namely:
1) The styptic <note>A substance that is contracting or binding; here referring to the "vitrifiable" or glass-making earth.</note> earth, which can be turned into glass, and which indicates the principle of salt or the element of earth.

2) A fatty, greasy, non-combustible earth, which takes the place of air.

3) A moist, fusible, arsenical earth, which represents mercury and water.

->§. 11.<-

In order to properly apply what has been said in general to the actual generation of metals, and to prove the theorem not without reason and experience, Becher <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a German alchemist and physician known for his theory of three earths, which laid the groundwork for the phlogiston theory.</note> leads us into the mines, where one encounters 1) quartz, 2) <term>Bestieg</term> <note>A mining term for the clayey or fatty layer found between the ore vein and the surrounding rock.</note> and pitch, and 3) arsenical vapors, and so forth.

And of these he writes that they are the three most important principles of metals. Namely:
1) Body, salt, and ashes are indicated by quartz, flux, and flint.
2) Soul, sulfur, and coal are shown by what the miners call <term>Moder</term> <note>Decayed organic matter or "mold."</note>, <term>Bestieg</term>, and pitch.
3) Spirit, mercury, and soot are called by him arsenical, sulfurous vapors and steam.

# Of the Golden Fleece

It is therefore evident that certainly the flint and hard stones, clay, loam, and whatever can be turned into glass, are the mothers of metals and minerals. <note>The "mothers of metals" refers to the host rock or matrix in which ores are formed, believed by alchemists to nurture the growth of minerals.</note>

That this matter, however, is permeated by an oily and phlogistic <note>phlogistic: relating to phlogiston, a hypothetical substance once thought to be the essence of fire and inflammability</note> essence, which pours into it and either joins with it, or from which mercurial, arsenical, and sulfurous vapors and fumes arise, etc., can be observed in all ore-pits and mines where metals and minerals either grow or bloom. <note>The terms "grow" and "bloom" reflect the early modern belief that metals were living organisms that matured underground.</note>

->§. 12.<-

In that incomparable polemic, which was arranged as a letter to Boyle <note>original: "Dissertatione Epistolica ad Boylum." Likely referring to a work addressed to the famous chemist Robert Boyle regarding the nature of matter.</note>, it is further explained that neither common mercury, nor sulfur, nor common salt—being dead bodies—contribute anything to the generation of metals. Rather, that which makes the metals within the ore veins, and their fundamental essence itself which penetrates the stones, is a subtle, sulfurous-mercurial—that is, actually an arsenical—earth, in the form of a sulfurous vapor, which is combined with a subtle earth. By this, one should not understand that general combustible principle, but rather a certain fatty, specified earth. <note>The author is distinguishing between common chemical substances and the "philosophical" or "subtle" principles required for alchemical transmutation.</note>

<summary>This page argues that common stones and clays serve as the "mothers" or host environment for metals, which are formed by subtle, vaporous essences rather than common chemical elements.</summary>

# First Section.

of its kind; for example, in gold a subtle yellow earth, in silver a white, shining one, in copper a red one, and in iron, tin, and lead a brown, blackish earth. This is what is called the <term>sulfur of metals</term> <note>In early chemistry or alchemy, "sulfur" referred not just to the chemical element, but to the principle of combustibility and color within a substance.</note>, and it is also found in many things that are not metallic, such as in earth, stones, and even in plants and animals.

And this is what moved Kunkel <note>Johannes Kunckel (c. 1630–1703), a famous German chemist and glassmaker who was skeptical of traditional alchemical terminology.</note>, who was not at all accustomed to philosophical figures of speech, to reject the sulfurous component and to admit nothing but salt and mercury in the formation of metals.

->§. 13.<-

But since we consider all the quarrels that arise in philosophy regarding the elements and components to be quite superfluous and vain, we shall not enter into a legal dispute with anyone, whether he may assume and maintain three or two metallic principles. For we accomplish little good with such disputes.

Even the decomposition of metals, which is undertaken with all kinds of corrosive, dissolving, and other such means, cannot decide the matter. Because it is not yet known for certain whether that which is [produced] either through fire, or through minerals, or other salty, sulfurous

# Of the Golden Fleece

these active things, produced as well by the dry as by the wet path <note>The "dry path" refers to high-heat crucible smelting, while the "wet path" refers to liquid chemical solutions.</note>, were initially the composite constituents of that metal from which they were derived; if they are not, when they are precisely joined together, they restore that metal again—which is possible in very few matters of the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or early chemistry.</note>.

We shall let it suffice for our present purpose to say that a single common nature, or a mercurial constant essence, exists for all metals.

Namely, a heavy essence that allows itself to be hammered and melted, is constant in the fire, and possesses all such properties by which the generally known metals are distinguished from other things, the existence of which no one will deny.

->§. 14.<-

Now this essence, which in itself is very pure and pours itself through all types of metals and minerals—which perhaps also, according to its kind, is found in plants, animals, meteors, and the like—is subject to uncommon variations; yet these are only accidental to this essence itself, and can be restored to purity again through the Art. ->§. 15.<-

Whether Aristotle understood this very same thing by the "First Matter" <note>original: "materia prima"</note> of metals, let those investigate who abuse his authority and absolutely deny the transmutation of metals. From this arises the question: can metals be reduced back into that matter which is common to all things? If this is answered with "Yes"—as a great number of philosophers unanimously testify—then it follows of its own accord that metals which have become pure and stable again are transformed among themselves; or rather, that when all differences have been removed, one obtains that pure substance, and when one again gives to it

### §. 16.

Just as nature itself, in the interior of our globe of earth and water, generates metallic beings through all kinds of vapors which enter into various earths, and destroys and transforms them again in manifold ways; so also can Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to Alchemy or human chemical skill as opposed to natural processes.</note> upon the earth transform metals primarily in two ways. Beccher <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous German alchemist and physician known for his theory of "earthy" elements.</note> wisely writes of this in the supplement to his *Subterranean Physics* <note>original: "Supplemento Physicæ subterraneæ"</note> in the sixth chapter: namely, through subtraction and addition, both particularly and universally.

Subtraction and "particularly" <note>term: particulariter (specifically or partially)</note> occurs when a certain part—either an earthy or a watery one (a salty or a mercurial one)—is separated from the metallic mixtures; by the removal of which, the remaining mixture is essentially changed in its character.

However, because it is very difficult to separate anything from their constituent parts due to the strong bonding of the metals (says Beccher), the method of addition is not only more feasible, but also more universal. Namely, to those

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical theories of Johann Joachim Beccher regarding the transformation of metals through the processes of subtraction and addition. It compares natural geological formation to the "Art" of the alchemist in altering metallic properties.</summary>

And through this addition, the entire mixture is fundamentally <note>The original German "wurzelhaft" implies a change at the very "root" or essence of the substance.</note> changed in its constitution.

We, however, maintain that there is yet a third general type of transformation, which depends solely and exclusively on the change of the shape and the position of the parts—that they either step closer together, or are otherwise united and formed together, without any foreign matter being added. Experience testifies that this is possible through motion and friction alone.

->§. 17.<-

This aforementioned common essence or metallic substance, however—whether one obtains it in a dry or wet form—is called by some "Mercury," and specifically "their" Mercury <note>original: "den ihrigen"; this refers to the "Philosophical Mercury," a theoretical substance alchemists sought to extract, as opposed to the common quicksilver found in shops.</note>, in order to distinguish it well from the common kind.

# Of the Golden Fleece

This, however, may be obtained in whatever way one wishes—either through removal or addition, or simply through the quickening salts within the metals, or by means of amalgamation with common mercury <note>original: "Quekſilber"</note>; yet it shows the way in which metals are mixed in their innermost being and permeated in their smallest parts. That is, they can be transformed among themselves no differently than how one water, when mixed with another, communicates its quality and nature to it.

### §. 18.

In our times, a treatise has been published in Saxony concerning the Mercury of Bodies <note>The "Mercury of Bodies" refers to the metallic essence extracted from solid metals, believed to be the key to transmutation.</note> and how it may be easily prepared with the help of oil of vitriol <note>original: "De Mercurio corporum olei Vitrioli beneficio facile parando"; oil of vitriol is concentrated sulfuric acid.</note>. The author or editor of this work, as is well known, was Reidhold, who is said to have learned this art from Kunkel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703) was a famous German chemist and glassmaker.</note>.

Reidhold himself writes to us that all copies of this booklet were bought up by envious people and withheld from public use. He was reminded by letters that he should never let this writing come among the people again in the future, as he was supposedly deceiving himself in his opinion.

## Section One.

And this is what Kunckel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703), a famous German chemist and glassmaker</note> finally realized in his *Laboratorio* in the chapter on Vitriol. Previously, he firmly believed that the mercury produced with the help of oil of vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note>—which dissolves silver—was the "mercury of silver" itself. He did not yet know that true oil of vitriol is nothing other than a liquid mercury whose superfluous moisture has been removed by added metallic calxes <note>metallic oxides</note>, and which can be brought back to life <note>restored to a metallic state</note> once it has been raised out of the salty water.

Meanwhile, he writes in that same place on page 181: "I wish to swear upon my honor through a certain experiment that this mercury—whether made from silver or with silver and oil of vitriol—is as noble in its quality as that which is made from silver through other salts."

This experiment, which Kunckel kept secret, was made known by his apprentice Neidhold, who writes the following in the preface of his book: "I tested my obtained mercury by amalgamating it with its silver and distilling it away again through a retort into a receiver filled with water. Then, every time—even when I had gradually amalgamated a pound of fine silver with these five drams <note>original: 'Quentgen', a small unit of weight</note> of mercury (which he writes he obtained from two ounces of oil of vitriol)—everything, after the removal of the mercury,

<keywords>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel, Neidhold, vitriol, mercury of silver, oil of vitriol, alchemy, distillation, amalgamation, metallic calx</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded with the initial stages of a chemical experiment involving the extraction of a specific essence. This page continues the description of the resulting "mercury" and its transformative properties.</meta>

# Of the Golden Fleece

without the slightest loss in weight or quality, the most beautiful ducat-gold <note>High-purity gold used for minting coins</note> remained in the retort <note>A glass vessel with a long neck used for distillation</note>. And even if such a color did not withstand the test of the cupel <note>A shallow, porous bowl used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead</note> in lead; yet let this be granted to me as a wonder of nature regarding this mercury. For I defy anyone to argue against me that such a mercury is not naturally more "gold-like" and excellent than that derived from common gold itself, since the latter does not perform in such a way. As I finally also learned, silver can be colored through in a similar manner in a short time and with little effort using the mere oil or spirit from which this mercury is made, whereas otherwise its power is considered only capable of turning silver into copper. All of this, while not proving a true transmutation of silver into gold, seems to contribute not a little as proof of the possibility of transmutation in general.

### §. 19.

Upon this depends a considerable part of the "fixation" <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> of that highly praised Son of Maia <note>original: "Sohn der Maja"; a mythological reference to Mercury/Hermes</note>, the mocker of all alchemists, concerning whom a pleasant dialogue with an alchemist who had summoned him can be found in the appendix of the *New Chemical Light* <note>original: "Novi Luminis Chemici"; a famous 17th-century alchemical text by Michael Sendivogius</note>.

# First Section.

For there will hardly be any other true stabilization <note>original: "Beständigmachung" – the process of making a volatile substance "fixed" or resistant to fire</note> of the same, the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> excepted, than that which is undertaken by means of mercury, or the salt of the bodies.

Of this kind is that which is described in the Saxon processes under the name *Everlasting Ore-Growth* <note>original: "Minera Perpetua"</note> by Doctor Püts.

However, the amateurs <note>original: "Liebhaber" – refers to "lovers of the art" or aspiring alchemists</note> will not find the art of turning the bodies, gold and silver, into mercury quite as easy as is commonly thought and as the circulated processes promise.

These writings all claim, indeed, that the entire business is accomplished through the help of salt of tartar <note>potassium carbonate</note> and sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note>.

The very famous Beccher <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous German alchemist and physician</note> writes that he became the possessor of the mercury of silver through this work in Vienna. Kunkel <note>Johannes Kunckel (1630–1703), a celebrated chemist and glassmaker</note> is said to have given a similar process to Charles XI, King of Sweden, and persuaded him that the matter was so easy that Kunkel's daughter could have distilled the mercury from the silver at least fifty times. However, various experiments have yielded only a very small quantity of silver and have scarcely proven the possibility of it.

# Of the Golden Fleece

Lukas Osiander <note>Lukas Osiander the Younger (1571–1638), a Lutheran theologian and physician</note>, our countryman and former Chancellor at Tübingen, who stood in great favor with Duke Friedrich <note>Friedrich I, Duke of Württemberg, known for his intense interest in alchemy</note> due to his experience in chemistry, boasted that he could produce the Mercury of Gold <term>Mercury of Gold: a purified metallic essence believed by alchemists to be the "first matter" of gold</term> within the span of eight days. His process is based upon this very same foundation of salt of tartar <term>potassium carbonate</term> and sal ammoniac <term>ammonium chloride</term>.

But a thousand people may attempt such processes before a single one obtains anything profitable from them.

And this was also the reason why Rolfink and Wedel <note>Werner Rolfinck and Georg Wolfgang Wedel, influential professors of medicine at Jena</note>, who were famous as great chemists, categorized the "Mercuries of Bodies" among the "chemical impossibilities." However, Beccher <note>Johann Joachim Beccher, a father of the phlogiston theory</note> seems to have sufficiently refuted them in his *Additions to Subterranean Physics* <note>original: "Supplementis Physicæ subterraneæ"</note>.

At the very least, we ourselves could assert the possibility of this, without any boasting, by pointing to one or two grains of Mercury that we have produced through various experiments. Just as Kunkel <note>Johannes Kunckel, a famous glassmaker and chemist</note> had to be content when he was able to silver-plate a gold guilder using only the paper with which he had wiped the neck of the retort <term>a glass vessel used for distillation</term>.

I am of the opinion, however, that a diligent chemist will easily be able to understand it through his very beautiful "peach-blossom" <note>likely a reference to a specific color change or "purple of Cassius" precipitate indicating the presence of gold</note>, if fate is favorable to you; just as this same magnificent sight came before my own eyes of its own accord.

# Section Two.

### Concerning Metallic Salts, Cinnabar, the Use and Benefit of Hydrostatics in this Matter, Tinctures, or the Souls of Metals, as well as Whitening and Reddening.

-> §. 1. <-

Because such great difficulty is linked to the task of turning physical bodies into mercury <note>The process of "mercurification," or reducing a metal to its primary liquid state</note>, those stable and perpetual ore-growths that can be used for this purpose are almost as rare as the Philosopher's Stone and the Great Work itself.

There were some, therefore, who held the opinion that common mercury would only require a hundredth part of metallic salt to achieve the consistency and density of a perfect body <note>A "perfect body" refers to gold or silver</note>; they preferred to turn metals into a soluble salt using water. This followed the principle: "The salt of metals is the Philosopher's Stone." <note>original: "Sal metallorum est lapis philosophorum"</note> Although even this can hardly be done correctly unless the mercury is first removed.

A Frenchman named Atremont <note>Likely referring to the alchemist d'Atremont, author of "The Tomb of Semiramis"</note> occupied himself with this work day and night, as he once [recounted] to Olaus Borri <margin>Olaus Borrichius, a famous Danish polymath and chemist</margin>

### §. 4.

There are, to be sure, some who do not judge such works very favorably, which is why Baron von Schröder <note>Wilhelm von Schröder (1640–1688), a cameralist and alchemical writer</note> reckons them among the **alchemical deceptions** and gives them the name **"Fool’s Tincture."** Yet he also reminds us of this: he leaves it to the judgment of the reader whether he might perhaps learn something from this folly.

However, Baron Kunkel von Löwenstern <note>Johannes Kunckel (1630–1703), a famous chemist and glassmaker</note> answers these critics in his *Laboratory* <note>referring to his "Laboratorium Chymicum"</note> on page 303: those are indeed foolish who, when they have thickened <term>Mercury</term> <note>the metal quicksilver, often used as a base for transmutation</note> in this manner, believe it to be a silver <term>tinctured</term> <note>transformed or dyed by an alchemical agent</note> by the quicksilver. But he considers those even more foolish who despise such works without further investigation. The Elector Augustus, of most glorious memory, added the following to the description of this work: *In this lie wonders not previously experienced; therefore thank God, and sin no more.*

->§. 5.<-

### Section Two.

### §. 5.

I do not know whether I may recount here what I once observed and saw, which bears some similarity to these works. Be that as it may, I wish to recount it truthfully and sincerely.

I once dissolved silver in a peculiar and special <term>parting water<note>original: "Scheidewasser"; a historical term for nitric acid, used to separate silver from gold.</note></term>. Now, when it was more than half saturated (with the parting water), and having been left standing on the table overnight in autumn, it deposited crystals and sheet-like pieces onto the bottom, which in luster resembled <term>selenite<note>original: "Frauen-Eiß" (Lady's Ice); a transparent variety of gypsum often used in historical contexts as a substitute for glass or for decorative luster.</note></term>.

When I saw this pleasant and unexpected phenomenon the following morning, I poured off the water, not doubting that these little sheets were a portion of my silver which I intended to recover. But the friend who was with me insisted that I place the glass in a gentle heat, in the belief that the crystals would be transformed into an oily substance. However, this opinion—which I certainly did not consider correct—was false. For after several days, the crystalline sheets were hard, and the spirits of the parting water which remained with them flew away upward in the glass in the form of a red vapor, bit by bit.

# Of the Golden Fleece

After I had once again reduced the small metal plates <note>original: "Blechlein"; likely thin sheets of lead or tin used in alchemical trials</note> to powder, I poured distilled vinegar over them, by which they were immediately dissolved so that nothing remained at the bottom; although overnight, some whitish "flowers" <note>term: flowers (flos); a chemical precipitate or sublimate that forms a powdery or tuft-like deposit</note> had settled.

On the following morning, however, no more flowers were seen at the bottom, but in their place, many yeast-like substances hung in the middle of the liquid, resembling spiderwebs and appearing somewhat dusty.

I therefore strained the vinegar so that it was cleared of its dregs <note>original: "Heffen"; refers to the sediment or lees</note>. I set it in a bowl in the middle of the table overnight, and again a whitish, talc-like matter settled—almost as much as the weight of the small plates at the beginning—which would not dissolve in the vinegar even with heat, but remained unchanged at the bottom of the glass. Therefore, I purified the vinegar several more times by straining it through blotting paper to remove all white and black dregs. ## Section Two

Overnight, the remaining vinegar evaporated, drying into a gray powder. Finally, when I melted this powder over the fire, it yielded ten grains of fine silver. <note>A "grain" is a historical unit of weight based on a single seed of cereal; ten grains would be approximately 0.6 grams.</note>

### §. 6.

To this section, we may also appropriately add what Kunckel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703) was a famous German chemist and glassmaker who served various royal courts.</note> calls "Playing Cards"—or those pleasant works involving crystals and metal solutions that refuse to solidify into stone—concerning which he says that something extraordinary is hidden therein.

Namely, he dissolves mercury in spirit of nitre <note>term: spirit of nitre (nitric acid)</note>. He separates the part that crystallizes in the cold and likewise keeps the remaining liquid solution. He does exactly the same with the solutions of other metals. Afterward, he pours the solution of one (for example, silver) that will not turn to stone into the crystals of the mercury—or vice versa—so that they can be mixed at will like playing cards, which is a pleasure to behold.

He promises that through these labors, everyone will learn so much that they will no longer be able to doubt the transmutation <note>original: "Verwandlung"</note> of mercury into silver and gold.

# Of the Golden Fleece

### §. 7.

Those who count that process of Monte-Snyder <note>Johannes de Monte-Snyder, a 17th-century alchemist known for his "Universal Medicine"</note>—already well known to everyone—among the processes of how to turn metals into salt, do not seem to be entirely wrong. For they destroy every metallic king <term>Regulus: the pure metallic heart of an ore after smelting</term>, for example that of gold, through the flux, or rather through the "stroke" of Theophrastus Paracelsus, which he called: "burn fat vervain" <note>original Latin: "Verbenas adole pingues"</note>, a method very well known today, from which they extract the salt and sulfur, which they afterwards use according to whether one has more or less experience and skill.

For Snyder himself, during his life, allowed many works and experiments to be circulated here and there among the people, and gave them to whoever requested them as samples of the transmutation, or at least the improvement, of metals; of which we have cited one or two examples in our *Vindication of Alchemy* <note>original Latin: "Vindiciis Nostris Alchymiae"</note> on page 148, which are quite extraordinary. And although he found few who could imitate them, because so much gold,

## Second Section.

Anyone who wishes to do so may investigate these matters; we shall not dwell on the recounting of them at this time.

There is also a new, anonymous author <note>original: "Schriftsteller"</note> who believes he has "thrown pearls before swine." With quite learned propositions, he claims to know from his own experience that the salt of silver, when melted with common mercury, transforms that mercury into silver. Conversely, he claims that metallic mercury melted with silver is transformed, in its entire weight, into gold.

However, whether this occurred with—or without—profit, he writes that he will only reveal this to someone into whose ears he can whisper it.

### §. 9.

No one has explained more clearly or learnedly how metals—once they have been turned into salt or otherwise made more subtle <note>refined or purified into a more potent state</note> and endowed with a certain penetrating or active power—can make others essentially like them and of the very same nature, than Beccher. This great scholar, in his *Second Supplement to Subterranean Physics* <note>original: "Physica Subterranea," a foundational work of 17th-century chemistry by Johann Joachim Beccher</note> in the fourth proposition, paragraph 55, makes the entire matter clear through an experiment, showing that

namely that gold or silver, when they have been made subtle <note>refined or broken down into a more active state</note>, can penetrate into the innermost parts of metals, and thereby change and transform them.

An example, he says, may be silver, which, when it is melted with copper, does not change the copper in the slightest, but rather behaves as if it had been laid down next to a dead body. Therefore, it allows itself to be separated from the copper without changing its powers or effects.

But dissolve this same silver in <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>nitric acid; literally "strong water"</note>, precipitate it with common salt or with its spirit <note>hydrochloric acid</note>, and sweeten <note>wash away the acidity from</note> the precipitate; then you will find this silver to be meltable, volatile, and highly penetrating.

Mix with this an equal amount, or even more, of tin-ash <note>tin oxide</note> or iron filings; place it in a copper sphere consisting of two parts, fill the lower part, seal the joints with lute <note>a clay-based sealant used to make vessels airtight</note>, and make a gentle fire so that the sphere glows red, yet does not melt. Leave it in the fire for several hours; then let the sphere cool and open it. That which is on the bottom, melt it, and bring it back through the flux; thus you will find your weight of the inserted sil-

## Second Section.

...you will have the silver back again, or you will find the upper copper lid or part so permeated by the spirit of this volatile silver that it largely passes the test as true and good silver. <note>The "test" refers to cupellation or acid tests used by early modern assayers to determine purity.</note>

For this transformation of copper into silver, one can provide no other cause than that which it receives from the salt, without which, as is well known, it would achieve nothing.

Before we proceed further with Beccher, <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous German alchemist and physician known for his theory of "earthy" elements.</note> we cannot remain silent about the fact that this very experiment was counted among the frauds of the alchemists by Mr. Geoffroy <note>Étienne François Geoffroy (1672–1731), a French chemist who presented a famous paper on alchemical deceptions to the Royal Academy.</note> in the Acts of the Royal Academy in Paris, 1722. He does not deny the effect described by Beccher—that is, that a portion of the upper lid becomes silver—but he explains the matter by saying that the salts had corroded the copper particles and carried them into the air, placing particles of the elevated silver in their stead.

However, if the matter were so, then Beccher must have lied; for he expressly states that after the silver-calx <term>calx: a powder produced by heating a metal, now known as an oxide</term> which remained on the bottom was reduced, the original weight of the silver was found to be present.

Which of the two is to be believed, experience must tell. But be that as it may, the penetrating power of <term>Horn-Silver<note>original: "Horn-Silber"; a naturally occurring silver chloride (chlorargyrite) which has a waxy, horn-like appearance and was highly prized by alchemists for its unique properties.</note></term> can finally be seen from it.

### §. 10.

In the aforementioned passage, however, <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous German physician and alchemist known for his theory of "terra pinguis," the precursor to phlogiston.</note> Beccher continues on this subject and says: One should dissolve the silver in <term>aqua fortis<note>original: "Scheidwaſſer"; literally "parting water," referring to nitric acid used to separate gold from silver.</note></term> or spirit of nitre, then draw off the solution with gentle heat until it reaches the thickness of a salt. This salt should afterwards be dissolved several times with the spirit of vinegar, always drawing it off <insert>by evaporation</insert> and dissolving it again. Finally, one does the same with the spirit of wine <note>original: "Wein-Geiſt"; ethanol or high-proof alcohol.</note>. At last, let the salt of silver remain in the spirit of wine without drawing it off. This spirit of wine—without any corrosive sharpness or violence—when poured drop by drop onto common mercury, makes it stable as if in an instant and transforms it into silver. And do not believe that no more silver is found than was originally in the spirit of wine. You will certainly find, if you perform the test correctly and look closely at the weight, a heavier silver; you will see that a noticeable quantity of mercury has been transformed into silver and has grown into it.

# Second Section.

The cause of this transformation is to be attributed solely to the penetrating essence of silver.

The same occurs with gold. When you dissolve this in <term>Aqua Regia</term> <note>original: "Königs-Wasser"; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acid used to dissolve gold</note>, and add mercury to it, and draw off the moisture until it reaches the thickness of a salt, then dissolve the salt again in wine vinegar, filter it, purify it, and thicken it anew so that it flows like wax, it will then transform any piece of silver—even one as thick as a <term>Thaler</term> <note>a large silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries</note>—into gold. However, it must only glow, not melt; and this results in an increase of the gold. I cite these experiments only so that one may clearly see that gold or silver do not change or transform metals as long as they are coarse and thick; rather, only when they have first been made subtle so that they can penetrate, do they immediately work upon the metals—depending on whether they are more or less penetrating—to change and transform them.

And this is the basis of all effects of the subtle waters, which are commonly called <term>Graduation Waters</term> <note>original: "Gradier-Wasser"; liquids used to purify or "grade" the quality of precious metals</note>.

Finally, he says further: it is therefore as clear as day how much depends on this refining and making subtle. And if nature, even when outside the right path, nonetheless shows the possibility, what would she not do if one were to work on the right path?

# Of the Golden Fleece

### §. 11.

When you possess <term>parting water</term> <note>original: "Scheidwaſſer"; typically nitric acid used to separate gold from silver</note>, such as the ancient sages distilled while preserving the mineral spirits, with which one can dissolve silver; then, once it has been saturated, it will no longer divide or tear apart any small metal sheets thrown into it. However, if one places the whole mixture in a gentle heat, one will perceive either no effect at all, or only a very slight one. Yet after several hours, the small sheets will all turn black. If the color has enough luster, then know that a true transformation of the prepared silver into gold has occurred. For when you take those black sheets out of their place and put them into common parting water—or even into the previous batch which was not saturated—that which has not acquired a golden nature will recede back into a liquid state. The gold substance, however, will settle to the bottom in the form of black, incomprehensible dust, which only with the help of <term>Vulcan</term> <note>A common alchemical reference to fire or the furnace</note> will cast off its Moorish skin and present the most beautiful gold before your eyes.

And this I can confirm with my own experience.

## Section Two.

Having spoken [of these matters], we cannot pass over in silence the manner and way in which even the <term>Archaeus of the Earth</term> <note>In Paracelsian alchemy, the 'Archaeus' is the invisible 'internal artisan' or vital force that directs the formation of minerals and living things.</note> operates in the production of natural <term>cinnabar</term> <note>mercury sulfide, the common ore of mercury</note> and similar stones that are impregnated with mercury.

For it is well known that those mercurial vapors are thickened by the acidic salt of sulfur—in such a way that smoke meets smoke—into that red mixture known by the name of cinnabar.

And this can be seen from the imitation of modern artists <note>referring here to alchemists and chemical practitioners</note>, who with little effort create a consistently uniform mixture, sold under the name of "manufactured cinnabar," by mixing one part sulfur and several parts mercury in a state of flux. They can just as easily dissolve that natural mineral or cinnabar again by adding substances that more readily absorb and take in the acidic salt, distilling it back into common mercury and sulfur.

There is also no lack of writers who record that both these types of cinnabar—or mercury, which has begun to be fixed into a brittle body by the acidic sulfur salt—can be made entirely permanent into a metallic <term>silver body</term> <note>the alchemical goal of transmuting mercury into silver</note> that allows itself to be hammered,

### §. 13.

For the sake of curiosity, we wish to recount a process from a manuscript regarding the stabilization of cinnabar, which was formerly performed at the Court of the Elector <note>likely referring to the Elector of Saxony or another high-ranking prince of the Holy Roman Empire</note> and which, as far as we know, has not been published until now.

One makes this cinnabar, however, from prepared sulfur and quicksilver <note>mercury</note> which have been largely made stable; these are afterwards boiled gently for several days under a fusible salt and are thus made so stable that when silver or

## Second Section.

Gold is added, four ounces to every half pound—that is, the yield increases by half.

The preparation of the mercury for this work is done as follows: One dissolves in <term>nitric acid</term><note>original: "Scheidwasser," literally "parting water," used to separate metals</note> four parts of sublimated mercury and one part of silver, each separately. After a gentle boiling for twenty-four hours, the solutions are poured together; these, once all moisture has been drawn off, are dried again into a red mass. As for the sulfur, let it be elevated with quicklime and allowed to seethe and boil in urine; when it has dried from that, elevate it once more with iron filings. After this, combine the elevated sulfur and the prepared mercury, and melt them into a single cake, in the same manner as one commonly cooks <term>cinnabar</term><note>a bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide, the common ore of mercury</note>. To this, once it has been divided into elongated pieces like nails, one adds salt which has been made to flow like wax through repeated dissolutions and thickenings. Everything is left together for several days in a fire which must be kept at specific degrees of heat. And when the <term>fixation</term><note>original: "Beständigmachung," the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> is complete, one takes out only as much as desired and purifies it in the ashes by means of lead.

I have not, it is true, performed this work with my own hand, yet it seems neither absurd,

->§. 14.<-

That <term>Eureka<note>original: "Ευρηκα" — Greek for "I have found it!"</note></term>, or hydrostatic invention of <term>Archimedes<note>The ancient Greek mathematician (c. 287–212 BCE) famous for discovering the law of buoyancy.</note></term>, has always seemed to me worthy of admiration and deeper investigation; by this means, he discovered the fraud a goldsmith committed on a royal crown, without the use of fire.

On the occasion of the lectures on experimental philosophy <note>In the 18th century, "philosophy" referred to what we now call science.</note> which I explained to the students for thirty-six years, I also found the truth of the principles of hydrostatics in metals. I soon observed that just as much gold is required to fill the space occupied by silver—that is, if one fills a small vessel with one ounce of silver, one would need two ounces of gold to fill the exact same space. Consequently, the density of gold and silver stands in a reciprocal ratio of their volume.

Therefore, I concluded further through the principles of hydrostatics, that the metal which is composed of gold and silver,

## Section Two.

Silver, when produced from pure metals, loses less of its weight in water the more gold there is present in the mass. For a half-ounce <term>ounce<note>A historical unit of weight; in the apothecary system, roughly 31 grams.</note></term> of gold loses only thirteen <term>grains<note>The smallest unit of weight in this system, based on the weight of a cereal grain.</note></term> in water through common experiments, which is one-third of the weight it held in the air. However, silver of the same weight in the air loses twenty-four grains when placed in water.

I have now performed all manner of chemical experiments by which one is accustomed to making silver dense and firm, so that it might approach the weight of gold. I had no doubt that it would become more similar to the nature of gold—or at least in regard to this property, the sister to her brother, <note>The author uses the alchemical names Diana (Silver) and Phoebus (Gold) to personify the metals.</note> Diana to Phoebus—the heavier it was made and the less space it occupied. Consequently, if fine silver, which contains no gold at all and previously lost twenty-four grains in water, were to lose only one <term>scruple<note>A small unit of weight, here used to indicate a significant reduction in displacement.</note></term> after being chemically prepared, it would have already brought a part of itself toward the nature of gold.

I do not doubt that most people will laugh at this conclusion and call it weak and poor. For they might say: does not lead lose less weight in water than silver? How then can one claim with any basis,

<summary>The author describes experiments using hydrostatic weighing to determine the purity of silver and gold, theorizing that increasing the density of silver brings it closer to the "nature" of gold. He anticipates skepticism from readers who might point out that lead is also denser than silver without being more "golden."</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatic weighing, specific gravity, alchemical transmutation, silver, gold, density, Diana, Phoebus</keywords>

# Of the Golden Fleece

that the silver, which has been made heavier, comes closer to the nature of gold than to the nature of lead.

This conclusion indeed has an appearance of truth, and I reached it myself long ago without anyone else’s reminder.

In the meantime, I answer: it is a matter of indifference to me whether you say that silver prepared in this manner approaches more the pure substance of lead or the substance of gold, because I can still assert the transformation <note>The author is referring to "transmutation," the alchemical process of changing one metal into another</note> even if little is gained by it, which is enough for a philosopher.

It is certainly correct that one could easily deceive in this way if someone were to melt lead into the silver. However, such a fraud will soon be discovered through the generally known tests of the assayers <note>Assayers were specialists who tested the purity of precious metals</note>, and none is to be feared if one works with one's own hand with the silver which has been purified by lead and saltpeter <term>Saltpeter: potassium nitrate, used here as a flux to remove impurities from metal</term>.

We also believe we cannot err further if we assume that the purest silver—which has been brought to the density and solidity of gold in those properties to be derived from this most perfect metal—differs from the latter only in color,

<summary>The author argues that silver purified and densified through alchemical processes effectively becomes gold in substance, differing only in outward color, and defends against accusations of fraudulent alloying.</summary>

### §. 15.

I formerly believed, for a particular reason, that the extraction of the souls <note>The "soul" or "sulfur" was considered the active, combustible principle of a substance in alchemy</note> had its use, at least in medicine, provided that one proceeded in such a way that the impure, repulsive, foreign, and earthy parts were separated.

Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a famous chemist and physician</note> and Polemoin <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary or classical alchemical authority</note> have strengthened me in this opinion; they write that the entire host of diseases must yield to these sulfurs once they have been improved. And although one cannot consistently prove this work, I could nevertheless allow some use of these sulfurs in medicine, since for medicine intended for the human body, they do not need to be permanent and stable in the fire.

However, I could not be persuaded that they contribute much to the transformation of metals. For experience has taught me that the required stabilization <term>Fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable in heat</term> either never succeeds, or succeeds only after a very long and indefinite time, and that not every metal which receives the color of gold immediately becomes gold.

<summary>The author discusses the medicinal benefits of extracting "souls" or sulfurs from substances, noting that while effective for healing, these extracts rarely succeed in the permanent transmutation of base metals into gold.</summary>
