### §. 16.

These latter thoughts have been confirmed by the work, *The Secret of Hermetic Philosophy* <note>original: "Arcanum Hermeticæ Philosophiæ Opus," a classic 17th-century alchemical text attributed to Jean d'Espagnet</note>, in which one reads the following words in §. XXXI. and so forth: The entire host of philosophers <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to alchemists or "natural philosophers."</note> plagues and torments itself with the art of extracting the tincture from gold, for they believe the tincture can be separated from the gold and, in such a manner, be increased in power. However, in the end, they merely thresh empty straw. For there is no possibility that the tincture of gold can be entirely and completely separated from its natural body. For among all elementary bodies made by nature, there is none that is as perfect, or more perfect, than gold. And its perfection comes from the strong and inseparable union of the pure and coloring sulfur with the mercury <note>Sulfur and Mercury were considered the two primary "principles" or spiritual building blocks of all metals.</note>, both of which are prepared by nature in the best possible way; their true and total separation is denied to Art <note>The "Art" refers to human alchemy, as opposed to the "Work" of Nature.</note> by Nature. If one extracts some constant moisture from the gold by means of violent fire or water <note>In alchemy, "water" often refers to acids or corrosive solvents like Aqua Regia.</note>, or extracts a portion of a melted or violently dissolved body, it cannot be considered a separation of the tincture. For the tincture remains with its body and in no way separates itself from it.

# Second Section.

This is the mistake that is unknown even to artists <note>In this context, "artists" refers to alchemists or practitioners of the chemical arts</note>. Even assuming that the **tincture** <note>The concentrated essence or "soul" of a metal</note> could be separated from its body, one must still admit that this separation is not possible without the destruction and corruption of both the body and the tincture, since the artists do them violence through the **smelting fire** <term>Schmelzfeuer: a high-heat furnace fire</term>, the destroyer of nature, or through **aqua fortis** <term>Scheidwasser: literally "parting water," nitric acid used to separate metals</term>, which corrodes more than it dissolves.

It must therefore follow that the body, robbed of its tincture and **golden fleece** <note>A metaphorical term for the essential gold-nature within a substance</note>, becomes entirely useless, resulting in nothing but harm and disadvantage to the artist.

They may ultimately attempt to project their tinctures onto **mercury** <note>original: "Quecksilber"</note>, or upon any other imperfect body, yet their hope fails them in a twofold manner. Firstly, because the tincture can neither penetrate nor color beyond the powers of nature and weight; therefore, no profit will result by which the costs and the loss of the robbed and useless body might be repaid. Thus, people become poor when they work to their own detriment.

Finally, such a miserable and powerless tincture, which has been taken to a foreign body, will yield no perfect, constant, and lasting essence, such that it [could withstand] the test as

->§. 17.<-

Even the extraction causes a greater difficulty, because the means of dissolution <term>menstrua: solvents used to break down substances</term> are rare which do not at the same time entirely dissolve the body.

And we know through experience that most people deceive themselves here, when they think that the gold is left white, when such salty dissolution—

# Of the Golden Fleece

of the or the Soul <note>original: "Sele" (Seele); here referring to the essential "sulfur" or coloring principle of a metal</note>, namely according to the fundamental principle. Rather, it must occur through transformation, through purification, subtilization, and exaltation, not otherwise than like cochineal <note>a red dye derived from insects</note>, which in the common way gives a red color that dyes to the highest redness and in a fair quantity. But when this red color is prepared with nitric acid <term>Scheidwaſſer: literally "parting water," used to separate metals</term> or spirit of urine <term>Uringeist: an ammonia-based solvent</term>, it will be exalted in such a manner and spread out in its tincture that it can dye many thousand times more than before. Such an exaltation now does not come from adding more substance of the cochineal to it, but rather that the tinting quality is exalted and distributed through the subtle spirit of the nitric acid or urine. Just so must a metallic tincture be exalted in its quality by a subtle, mercurial spirit in such a way that, while the previous weight of the substance remains, the tinting power of the gold is increased many thousand times.

# Second Section.

little particles or dust-motes, according to whose diversity the entire mixture receives its distinction and change.

Berigardus <note>Claude Guillermet de Bérigard (1592–1663), a French philosopher and scientist who sought to reconcile atomism with alchemy.</note> now posits the theorem that in every metal there exists a certain fabric of little bodies which can be united with gold, provided that which is contrary to it and consists of other parts is removed, and that which is lacking in essential similarity is replaced and added. And he says that this double power of separation and replacement resides in that powder which is called the tincture <term>Tincture: in alchemy, a potent substance or "medicine" capable of transmuting base metals into gold</term>. And this tincture effects change with an expansive quantity (for example, one part to a thousand parts) because even the smallest part is sufficient to change the mixture of bodies.

And we see this in antimony <note>original: "Spießglas"</note> and tin, the smallest part of which, when mixed into metals that otherwise allow themselves to be hammered ever so well, makes them crumbly and brittle.

Therefore, our author concludes from the opposite case: namely, that there could exist a medicine which either makes volatile things stable, or makes that which can be drawn and hammered brittle and crumbly, or colors white things yellow; for it is well known that the yellow color of gold is merely a coating of those few little bodies that can be separated from the rest of the fabric.

# Of the Golden Fleece

See Mr. Boyle’s <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), a founding figure of modern chemistry</note> experiment in the treatise on the *Origin of Forms and Qualities* under the title: *On the Way and Manner of Transforming Gold into Silver*. <note>original: "de Origine Formarum & Qualitatum, titulo, de modo transmutandi aurum in argentum."</note>

The very same is seen with copper, whose red color is merely a coating that can be drawn out from it, so that the body may be made white. And this is what Ludovicus de Comitibus <note>Ludovico de Comitibus, a 17th-century Italian chemist and physician</note> calls in his *Metallurgy* under the title: *A Metal from Copper Without a Name*. Which I myself not long ago here in England (says Becher) <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a German alchemist and physician known for the phlogiston theory</note> performed with Prince Rupert. <note>Prince Rupert of the Rhine (1619–1682), a soldier and scientist who experimented with metallurgy and invented "Prince's Metal," a type of brass</note> Such a mixture and composition is found in tin. For this reason it "cries" <note>The "tin cry" is a characteristic crackling sound made when bending a bar of tin, caused by crystal twinning</note>, and if only a few small particles are taken away, it becomes sweet, does not cry, and can endure the cupel. <term>Cupel: a shallow, porous dish used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead</term>

We can therefore rightly conclude that metals can be changed in their most prominent accidental properties—such as color, sound, malleability, their ability to withstand fire, and their weight—if one either takes from or gives to them some small particles, as may be necessary. Thus far Becher.

### §. 20.

Because Beccher mentions Boyle's experiment regarding the transformation of gold into silver in this passage, it will not be poorly done to set it down here entirely in the words of that very famous author, for the sake of those who perhaps do not own his works themselves.

In the cited passage, he describes the seventh experiment thus: "After the solvent <note>original: "Auflösungsmittel"; likely referring to a strong acid or 'menstruum'</note>, as he calls it, had been made very sharp, we took very fine gold, which we made meltable again with three or four times its weight in copper. # Second Section.

Whether I have sufficiently proven it in this manner, let others judge.

In the meantime, it seems to us that it is proven through this experiment that an excellent transformation has occurred in these metals, and that the composition and density neither of gold, nor much less of the other metals, is too firm as to never be capable of being changed with the help of art <note>In this context, "art" refers to alchemy or chemical skill</note>; consequently, this very fact deserves to be cited as a proof for the possibility of transmutation in general. **Boyle** <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), a foundational figure in modern chemistry who nonetheless explored alchemical possibilities</note> opines, moreover, that the color of gold could also arise from mixture, just as in the case of **cinnabar** <note>mercury sulfide</note> a red color is produced from white mercury and yellow sulfur, which in turn is separated with **alkalis** (I say also by means of **oil of vitriol** <note>concentrated sulfuric acid</note>, if you require a solid form) after the white mercury has been set free; thus, a very sharp solvent could dissolve the bonds of gold and, by changing the previous texture, simultaneously produce a new one, to which one owes the whiteness and other properties related to silver.

# Of the Golden Fleece

...mentioning it without a specific name; and since this art is known even to the coin-makers today, we cannot leave it untouched.

Although copper is not truly transformed into silver that can withstand the lead test <note>The "lead test" refers to cupellation, a process used to refine precious metals; if the "silver" were fake, it would oxidize away during this test.</note> by this very well-known method—which is commonly performed with that mineral poisonous spirit called arsenic—it still cannot be denied that such an excellent transformation occurs, behind which lies more than is generally known.

Even now, as I write this, I recall a couple of verses which I composed almost at the beginning of my chemical labors on the occasion of a certain task, which go as follows:

-> *Join naked Venus with shining Diana,* <-
-> *Let the Mercury of Venus give the Sun a garment.* <-
<note>original: "Nudatam Venerem nitidæ conjunge Dianæ, Det Soli veſtem Mercurius Veneris." In alchemical code, Venus represents copper, Diana represents silver, the Sun (Sol) represents gold, and Mercury represents quicksilver.</note>

That is to say: Combine the bare copper with the shining silver, and let the mercury of the copper give the gold the clothing of the copper.

I leave it to the judgment of the well-disposed reader whether this should be considered worthy of further reflection.

# Second Section.

### §. 22.

There are indeed all sorts of ways to make copper white, even without arsenic, which I wish to record for the sake of those who—not without cause—dread the poison of arsenic. In the meantime, if one handles it correctly, there is nothing to fear from it. Many processes are known.

We have the following from the manuscripts of Thortolassäus <note>Likely a reference to a specific alchemical author or pseudonym common in 17th-century laboratory manuals</note>, which has not yet been made publicly known.

Take copper that has been purified in the bath, and with a powder made for the most part of arsenic, saltpeter, tartar <term>potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used as a flux</term>, bolus <term>a fine, reddish clay used in alchemy and medicine</term>, and chalk, place it layer upon layer. Let it flow in the crucible, pour it out, add arsenic and the aforementioned powders again, and melt it so that it emerges from this bath with a wonderful whiteness and brightness, and gladly accepts that Diana <note>original: "Diana"; an alchemical code name for Silver</note> as a playmate similar to itself.

What further must be done so that the whiteness either remains permanent or is restored with salts is known even to common goldsmiths, so that it would be superfluous to write more.

We only add this: that among all arsenical processes, those are preferred by us above others

<summary>This page describes a specific alchemical recipe for whitening copper using a mixture of arsenic, saltpeter, and tartar, attributed to the manuscripts of Thortolassäus. The author notes that while arsenic is poisonous, it can be handled safely, and the resulting metal is compared to silver (Diana).</summary>
<keywords>copper whitening, arsenic, Thortolassäus, alchemy, metallurgy, saltpeter, tartar, Diana, silver imitation</keywords>

### §. 23.

But that one can make a coloring silver (<term>Argentum tincturale</term><note>original Latin: "Argentum tincturale"; a specialized silver used for "tincturing" or transforming other metals</note>) by the help of this arsenic, in order to combine it with naked Venus <note>The alchemical name for Copper</note> for greater profit, we learn not only from Holland, but it is also evident from the story of Sommer, which Baron Schröder describes, that this can also be done with quicksilver <note>Mercury</note>.

This Sommer was a Dutch goldsmith, imprisoned for a time in Vienna, whose masterpiece is recounted thus: He pressed one pound of quicksilver—which had been thickened with vinegar, salt, and verdigris <note>A green pigment or crust formed on copper; used here as a chemical agent</note>—through leather, so that six remaining ounces passed through. To the other ten thickened ounces, he added six drachms <note>A small unit of weight</note> of the arsenical medicine, and cemented them with oil and verdigris in a closed crucible for half an hour. Afterward, he removed the lid, strengthened the smelting fire, threw in finely pieces of copper, melted it for half an hour, and poured out six and a half ounces, eight lots <note>A traditional German unit of weight</note> of assay-standard silver, from which

# Second Section.

on the cupel <note>A shallow, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead.</note> nearly four ounces of the most perfect silver, as soft as gold, remained.

The medicine <note>In alchemy, the "medicine" or "tincture" refers to the substance used to trigger transmutation.</note> was prepared under the open sky in sixteen hours because of the poison of the arsenic, in which time the arsenic had to be made stable <note>original: "beständig." In this context, it means "fixed" or non-volatile, so it would not evaporate under heat.</note>. Within such a time, a single person working with a strong fire could cook twenty-five pounds of it into a stone, or into a red, stable glass.

Baron Schröder writes that he investigated and experienced all of this with his own hand.

However, Sommer never revealed the entire art of preparing the medicine perfectly to anyone, after he had been released by the Emperor due to a false report.

->§. 24.<-

Just as the transformation of copper into a metal without a name—which is similar to silver, since the brown color is turned into white—sufficiently proves a true change that extends even further; so one can no less find a proof for the possibility of the transformation of metals from another feat of craftsmanship, which is likewise generally known. or what is called Prince Rupert’s metal <note>A brass alloy consisting of about 75% copper and 25% zinc, named after Prince Rupert of the Rhine (1619–1682).</note>.

And just as that "White" is not true silver, so too is this so-called "Red" not made into true gold. For whoever would take this for gold would be another Glaucus, who exchanged golden armor for bronze with Diomedes, whose folly Martial <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis, a Roman poet known for his Epigrams.</note> describes when he writes: I believe, Glaucus, even you were not such a fool, you who gave golden things to him who gave you bronze.

> (I think you were never so stupid, Glaucus, <note>original Latin/Greek: "Tam stupidus numquam, puto, Glauce fuisti, χρύσεα donanti χάλκεα qui dederas."</note> who gave golden things to one giving you bronze.)

And yet today this piece of artistry is known to be just as useful as that by which, in the previous century, a merchant in Nuremberg named Lochner greatly enriched himself, by bringing this very beautiful metal to the point where it could be hammered and drawn out just like gold.

How brass is manufactured from copper can be read in all its circumstances in Erker’s *Underground Court* (**Erker’s Aula Subterranea**) <note>Lazarus Erker’s famous 1574 treatise on ores and assaying.</note>.

In this regard, it is remarkable that when the copper has been melted with calamine <note>A mineral ore of zinc (zinc carbonate), historically used in the production of brass.</note>, more weight results than the copper and calamine were before—

Francis Mercury van Helmont asserts in his seemingly paradoxical discourse <note>original: "in Paradoxali Discursu"</note> that metals, especially gold and silver, consist of a white sulfur mixed with a little red sulfur; or, what is the same thing, of common, white volatile arsenic and a little more stable substance mixed with red, which is called zinc. This zinc, when added to copper, gives it a far more beautiful gold color than when one uses calamine to make brass.

### Third Section.

However, many contentious questions arose concerning this experiment, and a large party claimed that in every type of clay <note>original: "Leimen"</note>, but also in flax, and in other plants, when they are burned and reduced to ashes in the smallest degree, actual metallic particles are already present here and there. Thus, Mr. Geoffroy <note>Étienne François Geoffroy (1672–1731), a prominent French chemist known for his "affinity tables."</note> also believed it was impossible to make ashes from herbs without simultaneously making iron, which could be discovered therein with the help of a magnet. Therefore, in the fifth year of this century, on the 9th of December, he did not hesitate to present this chemical question to the Academy of Sciences in Paris: Is iron found in ash that contains nothing but a few parts?

Mr. Lemery <note>Likely Nicolas Lemery (1645–1715), a famous French chemist and apothecary.</note> proved in the following year that even in honey—being the finest juice collected by bees from the flowers of plants—iron particles were present. And the younger Mr. Lemery <note>Louis Lemery (1677–1743), son of Nicolas, who continued his father's research.</note> claimed the very same regarding castoreum <note>original: "Bibergail"; a yellowish secretion from the scent glands of beavers, historically used in medicine.</note> when reduced to ashes, a subject derived from the plant kingdom. Lemery defended against Geoffroy and others the view that the very fine iron particles arise through the roots planted in the earth, and are effectively present in the herbs before they are turned into ashes. # Third Section.

of the art. Also the German proverb: He who knows iron and copper slag, can fill both bag and sack. <note>This proverb emphasizes that wealth can be found in the waste products of smelting if one possesses the right chemical knowledge.</note>

And this has been expressed by Johann Joachim Becher <note>A famous 17th-century German alchemist and physician, known for his role in the development of the phlogiston theory.</note> in his *Sand-Growth* (in *Minera Arenaria*) page 909, with a Latin verse:

(He who knows the slags of iron and of copper, and knows their use, can collect great wealth for himself without effort.)

I will not speak of the philosophical benefit and use, which is worth more than gold and silver, namely that through guidance and deeper contemplation of the same, we might perhaps see to the very bottom of the so deeply hidden nature of metals, investigate them, and thereby be able to know what may be a hindrance or a benefit to each one.

which, for this reason, appears in the works of Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> under the name "Little Man" <note>original: "Männlein"</note>, just as the softer iron shall be called the "Little Woman" <note>original: "Weiblein"</note>.

In Styria <note>A region in modern-day Austria famous for its high-quality iron ore deposits.</note>, the best is said to be prepared, which is the most suitable for the manufacture of swords and surgical instruments.

Smiths and locksmiths wish to make just such a kind in small quantities for their daily use, through the frequent cooling of glowing iron in certain juices of herbs, or by melting it with coals of hard wood, or adding the hooves of cattle and horses to it, or through <term>cementation</term> <note>A process of heating a metal in contact with a powdered reagent to change its properties.</note> and <term>stratification</term> <note>The layering of materials in a crucible.</note> with urine, salt, and soot; they enclose it in <term>potter's clay</term> <note>original: "Letten"</note> and apply a strong fire, so that the internal parts step closer together and become a core-like essence, or steel.

To make steel in large quantities, one requires a special vaulted furnace that has its own particular side-benches, and a fire that must be maintained for two days.

In this art, the famous Lords Priors at Frankfurt were perfect masters. Into such a furnace they placed pots <note>original: "Häven"</note> that were two feet <note>original: "Schuh"</note> high, but six inches <note>original: "Daumen"</note> in diameter, which were filled with powder of beechwood coals, cattle hooves, and salt in the proper proportion

### Third Section.

portion were mixed and filled. Into these, iron bars are placed, which are removed at the proper time, heated to a glow twice, cooled in cold water, and finally hammered. In such a manner, you will have the desired steel, which is useful for all purposes. However, just as iron is made harder through these processes—so that one can make files and similar instruments which themselves divide iron—so too can this same material be made softer, either with sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride, used as a flux</note>, tartar <note>potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking</note>, sulfur, or with cattle dung, onion juice, salt with pebbles, and the like. It can be prepared for various uses so that it may be changed according to one's pleasure. Namely, when whatever you desired has been engraved <insert>into the metal</insert>, it can be hardened again and polished, or preserved by being gilded.

Stahl had already made the entire artifice publicly known in July 1692 at Halle in Saxony. <note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734) was a famous German chemist and physician who developed the "phlogiston theory."</note>

He believed he was the first to have taken this path; by combining the flammable constituent <note>original: "brennbaren Bestandtheil," referring to phlogiston</note> with the universal acidic salt, although Kunckel and others, including Glauber, had made mention of this matter before him. <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel and Johann Rudolf Glauber were prominent 17th-century chemists/alchemists.</note>

The praised author presents the experiment itself in the first chapter as follows: "When I take 1) the **acidity of vitriol** <note>sulfuric acid</note> or of sulfur, namely the acidic spirit, or the so-called oil of these minerals; 2) some saltpeter, salt of tartar <note>potassium carbonate</note>, or just potash, fixed saltpeter and the like, and nothing else besides these but fire and a crucible; then in the time of a quarter-hour I have the **liver of sulfur** <note>a mixture of potassium polysulfides</note>, or the alkali impregnated with sulfur. When this is then separated through dissolution in water and by precipitation with acidic things, and melted together, it is, to all appearances, the very same sulfur which one collects from the mines. I do exactly the same thing, with the same method and speed, from tartar made into vitriol, as if from a double secret, and from saltpeter made into antimony <note>original: "Spießglas," an old term for the mineral stibnite or antimony</note>. The whole art consists in the union of each—"

Mr. Geoffroy <note>Étienne François Geoffroy (1672–1731), a French chemist known for his "affinity tables"</note> says thus: "I mixed one ounce of red-made and calcined <term>vitriol</term> <note>Iron(II) sulfate, heated until it turns red and loses its water of crystallization</note> with two drachms of <term>salt of tartar</term> <note>Potassium carbonate</note>; I allowed the matter to melt over a strong fire, and while it began to become liquid, I poured into it at various times one ounce of the best brandy. After the matter ceased to burn and began to give off a piercing smell of sulfur, I took it from the fire. The flame was bluish. And after it had become cold, the matter was yellow in some places and red in others, with a smell of sulfur or rotten eggs. I made a lye from it, over which I poured distilled wine vinegar, which was precipitated with burning sulfur, etc."

->§. 7.<-

But I already hear reproaches in advance: What should this contribute to the transformation of metals? What use are these artificial compositions of vitriols, cinnabar, mercury, arsenic, iron, sulfur, and so forth?

Hardly anyone could arrive at such objections except those who are entirely ignorant in this Art of the Sages <note>a common term for Alchemy</note>.

However, to satisfy everyone, we answer that the use and benefit of this active or "philosophical fire" in the art of making gold is highly recommended by all those experienced in the craft. From it, a most excellent mercury can be obtained—no less than that obtained from oil of vitriol <note>concentrated sulfuric acid</note>—indeed, some even claim that the Philosopher's Stone <note>original: "Goldmachende Stein"</note> itself can be made from it. Böttger’s tincture <note>Johann Friedrich Böttger, 1682–1719, the alchemist credited with discovering the secret of European porcelain</note>, which he presented to King Augustus in Poland, is said to have been of this kind, of which we have spoken in our *Defense of Alchemy* <note>original Latin title: "Vindiciis Alchymiæ"</note>.

And it is known for certain that one can remove flammability, make the white and red sulfur fire-resistant, and that the metals, gold and silver, receive an increase through this process.

Instead of a formal testimony, one may look to the praises given to sulfur by Rupert—a man highly experienced in this deep and high art and counselor to His Royal Majesty in Poland—and by Mr. Henkel in his doctrine of fire (<term>Pyrotologia<note>From the Greek 'pyr' (fire) and 'logos' (study); a treatise on the chemical effects of fire.</note></term>). They credit sulfur with the power to ripen, to upgrade in purity, and even to transform metals, whether in its raw or purified state.

# Third Section

### §. 8.

We believe from experience that the growth and multiplication of metals—or the so-called "Philosophical Trees of Diana" <note>An alchemical experiment where silver crystals precipitate out of a solution, resembling a growing tree</note>—are not improperly cited as evidence for the possibility of transmutation <term>transmutation: the alchemical transformation of one element into another, specifically base metals into gold or silver</term>.

Regarding this matter, we have presented a compelling example in the *Vindication of Alchemy* <note>original: "Vindiciis Alchymiæ"</note>. This was shared and reported to us by Borrichius <note>Olaus Borrichius (1626–1690), a Danish polymath and chemist</note>, who stated that it took place in Brussels, in the Netherlands.

For he testifies that Mr. Sansimon told him in all seriousness how a certain small powder had imparted such great power to the water poured upon it that, when mercury was thrown into it, silver shrubs grew. These, when melted in the fire, left behind the finest silver.

We are accustomed to showing such shrubs to spectators in our laboratory in two or even three different ways.

<summary>This page discusses the alchemical growth of silver "trees" as evidence for the transmutation of metals, citing a specific account from Brussels involving mercury and a transformative powder.</summary>

# Of the Golden Fleece

The first method was through various amalgamations <note>The process of mixing mercury with another metal, such as gold or silver, to form a soft paste.</note>, involving boiling and distillation, or gentle sublimation <note>original: "gelind erhöhen"; the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again.</note>. And by this method, at the beginning of this century—when we were imitating the work of Clett and subsequently working according to the poorly organized teachings of Gallander—we demonstrated a very beautiful display with gold, silver, and common mercury in a retort <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</note> to various people.

For we saw a flower, like the most beautiful red rose; it spread its broad petals, as if from a single center, all around the retort, while its stem barely protruded above its soil. But this same barely perceptible protrusion, which appeared in the middle like a dry stem, had a small golden-yellow ring that cast off rays like glass feathers; meanwhile, the remaining gold-earth <note>The residue or "calx" left after the chemical treatment of gold.</note>, upon which the reddish petals or thin flakes lay, distinguished itself more as a brown powder. We have no doubt that all of this was formed by chance and by means of a particular mercury <note>In alchemy, "mercury" often refers to a specialized solvent or the "spirit" of a metal rather than just the liquid element.</note> which extracted the purple color from the gold.

Then at another time, and with a different mercury, using only a common method, little trees and branched shrubs allowed themselves to be seen <note>A reference to "Diana’s Tree" or "Philosophical Tree," a dendritic growth of crystals resulting from a chemical reaction.</note>.

->§. 9.<-

The other method, which represents the common Tree of Diana <note>A "Tree of Diana" (Arbor Dianae) is a branching silver structure created by a chemical reaction, named after Diana, the alchemical symbol for silver</note>, is usually made when the solution of silver, which has been prepared with nitric acid <term>Scheidewasser: literally "parting water," the historical term for nitric acid used to separate gold and silver</term>, is weakened by being poured over with rainwater, and then a portion of mercury is thrown into it; it grows into similar little branches and appears to attract the particles of silver to itself with a magnetic force.

Similar displays, according to various proportions of the ingredients and almost countless processes known to nearly everyone, are described here and there, such that it would be far too extensive to recount them here; for nothing is easier than to imitate them.

It is also known that in underground ore mines—such as the one in our Black Forest near the Wittichen Monastery <note>A historic mining site in Germany known for silver and cobalt</note> which is mined with great yield—their aforementioned silver grows like threads both out of the cobalt ore, as well as an even more beautiful variety out of the quartz (**silica stone** <note>original: "Lapide Siliceo"</note>), just as in other

And behold, instantly all the metals grew like little gardens and little trees, which were elegantly distinguished by their shapes and colors, so that the gold and the regulus of antimony appeared yellow, silver and copper green mixed with yellow, the iron black, and the tin, mercury, lead, marcasite, and zinc appeared white to the sight.

The reasons for such growths and similar examples were likewise cited by <note>Wilhelm Homberg (1652–1715), a famous Dutch-born chemist and physician</note> Homberg in the previously mentioned passage; others seek the cause in the meeting of an "airy acid" with a "fixed alkali."

# Of the Golden Fleece

To this we answer: If anyone is able to impart a kind of life to those dead metals, he would not be far from the goal of the philosophers, who all proclaim that our metals, if they are to be fit for the work of transformation <note>original: "Verwandlung" – the alchemical process of transmutation</note>, must be made alive.

And since it is commonly doubted that they possess a life, these examples will at least be able to prove the possibility, even though the Adepts <note>Adept: a master of the alchemical arts who has attained the Great Work</note> do not proceed in this specific manner. Nevertheless, that which we have cited from Borrichius <note>Olaus Borrichius (1626–1690), a Danish polymath and chemist</note> he calls a real transformation and change of mercury into true silver.

There is also no lack of authors who teach how to ripen these shrub-like fruits, and indeed, how to make these growths themselves permanent into perfect metals.

Glauber <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber (1604–1670), a famous chemist known for "Glauber's Salt"</note>, who is the inventor of this experiment with the juice of flint <note>term: "Kieselsteinsaft" – likely referring to potassium silicate or "liquor silicum"</note>, testifies that within this mother-of-flint <note>original: "Kieselsteinmutter"</note> the ripening, improvement, and multiplication of metals occurs.

Here we wish only to briefly remark that flint is held by many to be the Stone of the Wise—that is, to be its primary matter.

### §. 12.

The experiments of Mr. Homberg <note>Wilhelm Homberg (1652–1715), a celebrated chemist and physician</note>—a man whose German blood was animated with an Oriental spirit, and who was a master in chemical labors—are too rational and too ingenious for us to pass over them in silence regarding this matter. He is also to be held in high esteem because he earned the applause of the great Prince, who in our times was known to the whole world under the name of the Regent of France <note>Philippe II, Duke of Orléans, who maintained a private laboratory</note>. And this we admired at the beginning of this century in France, and experienced here again after several years, when this mighty ruler of France had the grace to have our laboratory visited by Mr. Böcler, a professor from Strasbourg.

And although this insightful gentleman knew very well that such great riches were not to be obtained through chemistry as easily as through other political arts and legal disputes <note>The author refers to "Rechtshändel," implying the lucrative nature of law compared to the experimental costs of science</note>, as they are called, a certain man named Lavy reported soon thereafter that he had always remained a patron of the philosophical arts, and especially of chemistry.

# Of the Golden Fleece

The transmutation of metals <note>original: "Verwandlung der Metalle"</note>, which he wished to prove in the ninth year of this century, rests upon the operation of introducing the matter of light into silver. He commands one to take one or two half-pounds of silver and to part it <note>to separate metals using acid</note> so that one may be certain that no particles of gold are within it. Afterward, they are melted a hundred times, and each time left in a fluid state for one hour. If you then part this silver anew in its "water" <note>nitric acid or aqua fortis</note>, he promises that you will find a considerable quantity of gold within it, of which it possessed none before these meltings. Through this manifest proof, a portion of the silver—which in his opinion could be permeated by the matter of light—has passed into the perfection of the finest gold through this admittedly tedious labor.

### §. 14.

No less ingenious is Homberg's <note>Wilhelm Homberg (1652–1715), a celebrated chemist and physician to the Duke of Orléans</note> second experiment, through which he proves in an even shorter time that there are parts of a certain middle metal found within silver, situated between the two lights, which could be transformed into the nature of gold without difficulty. Namely, he distills horn-silver <note><term>Hornsilber</term>: silver chloride, which has a waxy, horn-like appearance</note> with the antimony regulus <note><term>König</term>: literally "king," referring here to the Regulus of Antimony, a metallic form of the element</note>, and when it has been driven off, he dissolves the remaining silver in the parting-

<summary>This page describes two alchemical experiments attributed to Homberg intended to transmute silver into gold by exposing it to prolonged heat or chemical distillation to introduce "light" into the metal.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, silver, gold, Wilhelm Homberg, matter of light, horn-silver, antimony regulus, parting water, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with instructions on the initial treatment of the metal; this page continues the chemical process and the author's theoretical conclusions regarding the maturation of silver into gold.</meta>

# Third Section.

nitric acid <note>original: "Scheidwasser"; literally "parting water," the historical term for nitric acid used to separate gold from silver</note> so that the gold-calx <note>original: "Goldkalk"; a term for the powder or precipitate resulting from the chemical treatment of gold</note> may settle.

He says that when one repeats this very same process, one obtains very little, and the third time, no more gold at all from it. Finally, he concludes that there is a matter found in silver which is on the way to becoming gold, and which is still pale within the gold. And this matter can obtain the proper color through the fire. These, he says, are the two matters which constitute a metal that is between gold and silver, or a middle metal, which must be brought to the perfection of gold merely through smelting.

If this matter behaves in such a way, then little comfort will remain for our particularists <note>term for alchemists who sought "particular" transmutations of specific metals rather than the universal "Philosopher's Stone"</note>, because according to this opinion, they cannot obtain more gold from the silver than the amount which nature itself has prepared and destined for this path of transformation.

### §. 15.

It is nowadays a well-known thing, familiar to all who are even slightly versed in the art of separation <term>Scheidkunst: the early chemical science of analyzing and separating metallic ores</term>, that when they are told to perform ore assays with lead, they must, above all things, place that lead—with whose bath they wish to wash the metals—into the cupel <note>original: "Kapelle"; a shallow, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead</note> alone,

<summary>The author discusses the theory that silver contains a "middle metal" destined to become gold through heat, while warning alchemists that they cannot extract more gold than nature has already provided. He then begins describing the practical assaying process using lead and cupellation.</summary>
<keywords>nitric acid, gold-calx, transmutation, particularists, assaying, lead, cupellation, silver maturation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of metallurgical assaying techniques, specifically focusing on the behavior of lead and silver during cupellation.</meta>

# Of the Golden Fleece

so that after the melting, if any silver is still mixed with it, it leaves behind its grain of pure and perfect metal upon the cupel <note>A "cupel" (Kapelle) is a shallow, porous dish used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead.</note>. And this is afterwards weighed very punctually, so that the quality of the ores can be calculated and determined with great precision.

Now, however, it is also known that when that lead, which has been turned into glass <note>referring to litharge, or lead oxide, which forms a glassy substance during heating</note> in the ash-fire, is reduced back <insert>to its metallic state</insert>, it yields more silver than was initially found according to the proportions of that same grain.

Therefore, one of two things must occur here: namely, either a transformation <note>transmutation</note> of a portion of the lead into silver, or the ash-fire must have drawn into itself some of the perfect metals along with the imperfect ones during the assaying and parting of the ores.

To investigate this more closely, it will be well worth the effort to apply all diligence in the work itself, to leach the ashes, and also to be punctual in the preparation of the other necessary materials. For these things sufficiently teach us that important transformations occur through this reverberatory fire <note>a furnace that reflects heat onto the material rather than applying it directly</note>, as evidenced both by the increased weight with the lead of the ash-fire, and the decreased weight of the lead after it has been reduced back.

# Third Section

Indeed, there is no shortage of those who, in this manner, wish to bring forth the true, living gold-seed <note>original: "Goldsamen"; a term for the concentrated essence or "semen" of gold believed to be capable of growth</note>, or the transforming gold tincture, out of the ash-fire. This is especially so since, according to the teachings of Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and philosopher</note>, it has previously been held together eleven times with lead, or otherwise brought into a glass like a ruby with the regulus of antimony <note>original: "Spießglaskönig"; the metallic heart or "king" extracted from antimony ore</note>, from which a tincture can then be drawn.

### §. 16.

When experience confirms what we read here and there concerning the rebirth and transformation of salts—namely, that every kind of salt can be transformed into another salt of a different nature without great difficulty—it will certainly be an object worthy of consideration by the philosophers <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to alchemical practitioners or "natural philosophers"</note>.

And it is not unnecessary, be that as it may, to cite it here, since salts are commonly reckoned among the "lesser minerals" according to the common parlance.

The writers promise, however, that when the salt which one wishes to change has been robbed of its spirit <note>original: "Geist"; the volatile or gaseous component of a substance</note>, or its volatile part, and takes into itself the volatile part or the spirit of that salt which you wish to create, it will then become that very salt whose spirit you have added to it.

# Of the Golden Fleece

And upon this foundation, it is said, rests that universal solvent, or the <term>Alkahest</term> <note>A hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary matter.</note> of faith.

->§. 17.<-

A significant proof upon which the possibility of the **transmutation of metals** may be based, it seems to me, is that which is derived from the similarity to the **petrifaction of wood**.

Through this, the manner in which the **Philosopher's Stone**, or the transforming **tincture**, can act within the metals appears to be clearly demonstrated.

For, to use the words of a very famous man, "just as the sap which transforms into stone—when it penetrates into the openings and pores of wood and other bodies—gains such great power that it petrifies the bodies themselves, even though they are far removed from the nature of stones (and we know that this often happens in a short time and within a few hours); why should not, in the same way, an **elixir** or a **chemical medicine**—which has been brought to the highest subtlety and raised to perfect stability—pass through all the pores of the imperfect metal (which stand open during melting)?"

<summary>This page argues for the possibility of alchemical transmutation by comparing it to the natural process of wood turning into stone (petrifaction). It suggests that just as mineral-rich water can penetrate and harden organic matter, a refined alchemical elixir can penetrate and transform base metals during smelting.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation of metals, petrifaction, Philosopher's Stone, tincture, alkahest, elixir, chemical medicine, alchemy</keywords>

### §. 18.

For the sake of curiosity, I wish to add another experiment which I previously allowed a certain artist—who was quite poor, as is the common state of such people—to perform in my <term>laboratory|Laboratorio: a workspace equipped for chemical and alchemical experiments</term> entirely at my own expense, in order to show me his "art," as he expressed it. He proceeded in this manner: First, from two ounces of <term>calcined ore|gebrannten Erzes: ore that has been heated to a high temperature to remove volatile substances</term>, by means of a <term>distilled vinegar|deſtillirten Eſſigs: a common alchemical solvent used to extract salts or essences</term>, there arose sky-blue, very beautiful crystals. He ground these, mixed them with half an ounce of <term>horn silver|Hornſilber: silver chloride, often appearing as a waxy, horn-like mineral</term>, and by increasing the heat, produced yellowish <term>flowers|Blumen: a term for the powdery sublimate or solid residue that forms after heating a substance</term>. And when he threw these into a <term>drachm|Drachme: a unit of weight, roughly 3.7 grams</term> of molten silver and poured it all out, he obtained two drachms of somewhat pale gold. However, because this gold was not accurately examined and <term>tested|probirt: subjected to a cupellation or acid test to verify purity</term>, I can say nothing further about it.

But since this experiment can be performed by anyone who is not entirely inexperienced in these works,

### §. 19.

Another individual made an almost similar attempt with iron in our very same laboratory, also at our expense, and not entirely without good success.

He stated, however, that he had arranged the matter thus: He elevated <term>sulfurated iron filings</term> <note>iron filings treated with sulfur</note> several times with <term>arsenic</term>, <term>sublimate</term> <note>mercuric chloride</note>, and <term>antimony sulfur</term> <note>antimony trisulfide</note>. He afterwards mixed the powder into silver by means of <term>cementation</term> <note>a process of heating a solid metal surrounded by a powdered reagent</note>, and finally tested everything through the <term>cupellation</term> <note>original: "Bleiprobe" - the process of refining gold or silver with lead</note>. And so, from half an ounce of this silver—which was dissolved in <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "Scheidwasser" - nitric acid used to separate gold from silver</note> and from which all gold had previously been separated—he obtained three and a half grains of the most beautiful and finest gold.

Although this experiment yielded no profit <note>the cost of the materials exceeded the value of the gold produced</note>, it nevertheless sufficiently proves the reality of the <term>transmutation</term> <note>original: "Verwandlung"</note>.

After this, the remainder that stayed behind following the evaporation of the rainwater is divided through further distillation into a volatile salt and oil. ### Third Section.

Among several stories, that of the Argonauts <note>The mythological heroes who sailed with Jason to find the Golden Fleece.</note>, or the history of the Golden Fleece—which we explained at the beginning—and the adultery of Venus with Mars, can easily be interpreted in this sense <note>The author suggests these myths are allegories for the union of chemical substances.</note>.

And since a manuscript of a Rosicrucian <note>A member of a secret philosophical and mystical society (the Rosicrucians) active in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note> came into our hands some thirty-odd years ago, wherein a process is mysteriously described in the form of a poem or an Ovidian fable <note>Referring to the style of the Roman poet Ovid, known for his "Metamorphoses."</note>; the well-disposed reader will not find it burdensome to read such as a conclusion.

We share it faithfully, exactly as it reads. It reads as follows: Venus, who is said to have been Vulcan's <note>The god of fire and metalworking.</note> lawful wife, was once entirely inflamed by a fire of love to embrace Mars. She revealed this desire of hers to Mars and, so that the deed might remain more successfully hidden from her husband, she invited him into a cave in the earth. Mars, who felt this same lure of love, accepted this message with joy and betook himself to the designated cave to fulfill his and his beloved's wish.

### Third Section.

G 5

Of this man <note>Likely referring to Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), a prominent chemist mentioned in the preceding context</note>, we know that he is held in higher regard by the majority of today's <term>natural scientists<note>original: "Naturkündiger"; those who study the laws of nature</note></term> than Lullius <note>Ramon Llull, a 13th-century philosopher associated with early alchemy</note> or the entire host of ancient philosophers. This same man, however, in his *Foundations of Chemistry* <note>original Latin: "Fundamentis Chemiæ"</note> at the end of the book, expressly asserts the possibility and correctness of the <term>art of transmutation<note>original: "Verwandlungskunst"; the alchemical process of changing base metals into gold</note></term> as follows: he confesses and believes that, next to Divine Providence—which reveals itself quite uniquely in this work—the matter itself, or the transformation of metals into gold according to the philosophical view, is actually in itself of less significance than the sowing and planting of grain, or the kneading of a bread dough. Its moral value and utility, however, are highly estimable to him who uses it rightly, and highly harmful to him who abuses it.

Whoever wishes to read histories of transmutation will find several collected in our *Vindication of Alchemy* <note>original Latin: "Vindiciis Alchymiæ"</note>, to which many more could still be added.

Among those, however, whose credibility can hardly be called into doubt, the transmutation history of the still-living and very famous Count von Würben <note>Count Rudolf von Wrbna/Würben, a high-ranking Bohemian official and known patron of alchemy</note> deserves to be cited; it has been legally confirmed and was only recently described through correspondence.

<summary>This page discusses the essential properties of gold, specifically arguing that density (gravity) is its defining characteristic, and expresses skepticism toward the possibility of alchemical transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>gold, cupellation, specific gravity, alchemy, transmutation, Cicero, density, chemical properties</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 107, which likely discussed the physical properties of metals and the theories of the chemist Friedrich Hoffmann regarding the impossibility of transmutation.</meta>

->Of the Golden Fleece<-

Because, by his own admission, the transformation of weight depends upon the change in the texture of the parts <note>original: "Geweb der Theile"; referring to the internal molecular or atomic structure</note>. However, the texture of the parts can also be changed in an instant merely by fire.

And of this, many examples exist in nature. Therefore, when such a medicine <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or "Tincture" in its medicinal aspect for metals</note> passes through the pores of the metal melted in the fire like a flash of lightning—as the philosophers <note>The Alchemists</note> explain it—then indeed a great change in the texture of the parts can occur in an instant; the parts can step closer and more firmly together, and thus the metal can be made denser and, consequently, heavier.

For weight depends upon a greater number of parts within the same volume, or what is the same thing, upon the number of these same parts within a smaller circumference. But are there not many examples of the thickening <note>densification</note> of bodies, when the same matter is retained but brought closer together into a narrower space?

Hoffmann’s <note>Friedrich Hoffmann (1660–1742), a famous German physician and chemist who was often skeptical of alchemical claims</note> proof only concerns those who believe that this work can be accomplished through the mere extraction of a tincture and casting it upon a body that has not been prepared.

<summary>This page argues against the skeptic Hoffmann by claiming that alchemical transmutations are possible through the rapid densification of a metal's internal structure when treated with a "medicine" in the fire.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, transmutation, density, Friedrich Hoffmann, texture of parts, metallic transformation, tincture</keywords>

### Third Section.

This is not the opinion of the Adepts <note>Adept: a master alchemist who has successfully discovered the Philosopher's Stone</note>. They possess a mercury which is heavier than gold itself, and from which they also prepare their tincture <note>Tincture: a potent alchemical substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold</note>; this tincture, however great and important it may be, can be called nothing more than a highly refined or "cooked" mercury.

And the famous Mr. Boerhaave <note>Herman Boerhaave (1668–1738), a renowned Dutch physician and chemist who conducted extensive experiments on mercury</note> must have noticed something of this when he spoke of a mercury that had been distilled 511 times, thereby coming closer to the weight of gold. He asks: whether mercury, if worked upon further, could finally be thickened into the weight of gold? Whether it would then be a "living gold," or the "Mercury of the Wise" <note>original: "Mercurius Philosophorum"; the essential, animated starting material of the Great Work</note>?

Furthermore, let the learned man consider, if he pleases, how closely those bodies upon which one usually performs projection <note>Projection: the final stage of alchemy where the "Stone" is cast upon molten base metal to transform it</note> are related to the weight of gold—especially purified mercury, even lead, and the remaining metals, regarding their mercurial part, which is solely and uniquely the proper and first matter of this transformation. And so he will easily notice the difference as to why other bodies do not, in this same manner and with such ease, actually and truly convert their specific weight into another.

<summary>The author discusses the alchemical theory that mercury can be refined until its weight equals or exceeds gold, citing the experiments of Boerhaave to support the possibility of creating the "Mercury of the Wise." It argues that the mercurial component within metals is the true key to their transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>mercury, gold, Herman Boerhaave, tincture, weight and density, Mercury of the Wise, projection, transmutation of metals, first matter</keywords>

<meta>Continuity from page 109: The text continues the discussion on the density of substances and the philosophical stone. Catchword "praised" (rühmte) connects to the final sentence of this page.</meta>

# Of the Golden Fleece

One can make oil of vitriol <note>Sulfuric acid</note> as clear as water, which is nothing other than a fluid mercury, and is almost as heavy as gold itself. And this is a clear proof that it either already possesses the pure mercurial essence—if I may speak thus—or is not far removed from the actual weight of gold.

We also spoke above about the ways to make silver more fixed <term>fixed: making a substance resistant to heat or evaporation</term> and to bring it closer to the weight of gold, which methods, however, stand far below the power of the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>. For the latter, when it permeates the metals in flux, can in an instant unite the parts essential to itself, so that at the same time the adverse parts are separated. And I do not see why this work and effect should be impossible, which a certain Mr. Hoffmann <note>Likely Friedrich Hoffmann, a famous 18th-century physician and chemist who was often skeptical of alchemical claims</note> could certainly doubt with reason, as the words of the commentary fortunately state.

1) One finds such <term>Adepts</term> <note>A master who has successfully discovered the Philosopher's Stone</note> nowhere; instead, as many as are running about are merely poor <term>Alchemists</term> <note>Here used disparagingly for "gold-cookers" who fail to achieve the Great Work</note>.

# Of the Golden Fleece

...has been attempted in vain by even the most skillful artists.

To the first point, we answer: whether any **Adepts** <note>Adept: a master who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note> are living at this time, we do not truly know. For almost only the Adepts themselves can know this through a special art.

However, we have learned from their writings that they admonish the **Sons of Art** <note>Sons of Art: a traditional term for students or practitioners of alchemy.</note> not to boast publicly, and not to use their treasure for luxury, nor even to reveal the secrets to their own children; but rather, they should behave as if they were poor, should let themselves be satisfied solely with the knowledge of God, and should be enemies of all worldly vanities.

Furthermore, anyone with even a little understanding will know how to distinguish between alchemists, <insert>deceivers</insert>, and philosophers.

Concerning the second point, we admit it has a great appearance of truth. For nothing is lacking here, neither the necessary means nor skillful techniques. And therefore, we can answer nothing else to this doubt than that one must recognize God's special **Providence** in these holy matters, which gives or takes away whenever it wills.

## Section Three.

The third point, although it may seem significant to the inexperienced, concerns us very little; for we have long known from experience what a great difference exists between knowing and doing. For even the smallest circumstance, which one either did not know or has neglected, gives the entire work a different turn. Not to mention that among a thousand people, scarcely one is skillful enough to continue works of this kind for a long period of time. So much for this time.

# Fourth Section.

### Concerning several useful cautions in the art of making gold, especially by the Particular Way. <note>The "Particular Way" refers to alchemical methods intended to produce a specific, limited amount of gold or silver, as opposed to the "Universal Way" of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

-> §. 1. <-

Not so long ago, in the three previous sections of our academic dissertation on the *Golden Fleece*, we submitted the possibility of the transmutation of metals to a three-day public examination by the opposing gentlemen, and proved such a thing both logically and through experience (both from cause and from effect). <note>original: "tam a priori, quam a posteriori"</note> No doubt remains for us in this matter, provided that we adhere to the description (definition) given at the outset.

However, we do not wish to conceal the fact that in such labors and the execution of philosophical processes, difficulties arise—both in regard to the laboratory workers (who often behave like a donkey trying to play the bagpipes) <note>A proverbial expression for someone totally unsuited or unskilled for a task.</note> and in regard to the works themselves, which are either poorly prescribed or wrongly understood; and furthermore, in regard to the diversity of materials, which may be the same in name, but are not the same in quality,

### §. 2.

To describe all of these errors—which not only beginners but even masters can commit—would be too extensive, for they are innumerable. However, we cannot refrain from citing a few as examples; furthermore, we believe that describing them will not be without utility. From these few instances, the lovers of such things <note>The term "Liebhaber" (amateurs/enthusiasts) often referred to devoted students of alchemy or the "Hermetic Arts" during this period.</note> will be able to perceive how difficult the journey to Colchis is <note>original: "Reise nach Colchus"; a mythological reference to the arduous quest of Jason and the Argonauts for the Golden Fleece, a common alchemical metaphor for the Great Work.</note>, and how boldly it is undertaken by those who are not even familiar with the necessary escort. Even the most cautious and experienced man must confess that it is the most dangerous path of all, upon which one can scarcely move a foot without falling.

### §. 5.

We once saw an artist <note>In this context, "artist" refers to an alchemist or practitioner of the chemical arts</note> who, with the help of iron, wanted to make a gold-bearing silver. He believed this agreed with the sayings of all the Sages, especially Geber <note>Latin: "Geber," the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy</note>, who writes: *Iron shall be your help.* And to this same end points that ancient *Knightly War of Sternhals* <note>original: "Bellum equestre Sternhalsii," a famous 16th-century alchemical allegory describing the struggle and union of metals</note>; if only he knew how to combine the iron with the silver in the flux <note>term: "Fluß" (Flux), a substance added to promote melting</note> so that it would enter into it and become one single metal with it. He even brought the matter so far that he actually achieved it by means of a very well-known flux, the so-called flint-stone, or the "glass of three" <note>term: "Glas von dreien" (glass of three), likely referring to a specific fusible compound of three ingredients used as a solvent</note>. And when he afterwards dissolved this metal in *aqua fortis* <note>term: "Scheidwasser" (parting water), nitric acid used to separate gold from silver</note>, he saw, not without joy, much black powder <note>term: "Kalk" (calx), the powdery residue left after a metal is burnt or dissolved</note> precipitate itself—namely, as much as the iron he had added. Thus, he could have easily sold this trick for a great deal of money to the inexperienced, who would have been of the opinion that such a powder was true gold.

### §. 6.

Above, we cited an experiment with mercury and silver from Kunkel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703), a famous chemist and glassmaker</note>, of which he writes that it is wondrous: if the separation is performed in nitric acid <note>original: "Scheidwaſſer"; literally "parting water," used to separate gold from silver</note> before the cupellation <note>original: "Kapellprobe"; a refining process where ores are heated in a shallow cup to separate precious metals from base metals</note>, a reddish powder precipitates which can be called neither gold nor silver. But if you wash this same substance in a lead bath, you will find that what has not been consumed remains a silver soluble in nitric acid.

# Fourth Section.

How deceptive such works are, however, and how completely impossible it is for a truly stable essence to be established—such as those metals possess which are made by nature and not by art—we learn from Alexander von Suchten <note>A 16th-century physician and alchemist known for his work on antimony.</note>. He believed he had extracted true gold and silver from the Regulus of Antimony <term>Spießglaskönig: the metallic heart or "king" produced by smelting antimony ore</term>, only to find that it was sophisticated <note>In this context, "sophisticated" means fraudulent or adulterated; a counterfeit metal that mimics the appearance of gold.</note>, even though it had already passed a common assay. This material, when he added the Mercury of Antimony to it, turned back into a volatile quicksilver which could not withstand the fire of the furnace or the crucible.

This is also confirmed by Beccher <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous German chemist and physician.</note> himself in his *Chemical Concordance or Commentary on Montesnyder* <note>original: "Concord. Chym. seu Commentario. ad Montesnyderum"</note> in the 13th paragraph. He cites the reason there: that such "stabilized" mercuries had not yet sufficiently mixed themselves with their earth.

For in this way, their earthly nature is gradually taken away from them, and the mercurial part <note>The "mercurial part" refers to the volatile, spiritual, or transformative essence within a substance.</note> is made stable and pure. For glass has the property that it grants entrance to all things, keeps metals in a state of flux, and makes volatile things and mineral vapors stable. Therefore, the ancients not without reason called it the Cement of Wisdom, the Hermetic Seal <note>original: "Hermetiſche Sigill"; a reference to airtight sealing, named after Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. And shortly thereafter he adds: "Seek your sulfur of gold <note>original: "Gold-Schwefel"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of a metal.</note> in things wherein it is abundant and superfluous; make it subtle, pure, and fluid, like quicksilver <note>mercury</note>. Then, do not go with it immediately to the cupel <note>original: "Kapelle"; a shallow, porous cup used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals.</note>, but first make it stable and corporeal with a good glass. And only then perform the test with it."

# Fourth Section.

### §. 11.

But whoever wishes to know whether Suchten <note>Alexander von Suchten (c. 1520–1575), a chemist and physician known for his work on antimony.</note> and Becher <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a famous German alchemist and physician.</note> have written the truth, he must prepare the Mercury of Antimony <term>Mercury of Antimony: the "spirit" or essential metallic principle extracted from antimony</term>, the preparation of which the famous Mr. Stahl <note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), the influential chemist who developed the phlogiston theory.</note> specifies as reliable in his *Foundations of Chemistry* <note>original: "Fundamenta Chymiae"</note> on page 213. "One should sublimate <note>original: "erhöhen" (to elevate); in this context, to vaporize a solid into a powder via heat</note> the Regulus of Antimony <term>Regulus of Antimony: the pure metallic form of the mineral antimony</term> with four times as much Sal Ammoniac <term>Sal Ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used as a flux and solvent</term> that has previously been sublimated with common salt alone; rub the rising flowers <note>original: "Blumen"; the fine powder produced by sublimation</note> once again together with that which remained at the bottom. Keep the thick residue in a gentle digestion-fire <note>a low, steady heat used to encourage chemical reactions</note> for two or three hours, just warm enough so that the Sal Ammoniac smokes only a little in the vessel; thus, with a gradually increased but not overpowered degree of fire, the sublimation may be completed. One should break the vessel, take it out, and preserve what has risen. If, however, it has left a residue on the bottom, one should again rub together four times as much Sal Ammoniac, set up the sublimation as before, and repeat the work until the entire Regulus that was taken for it is sublimated in this manner. With these flowers, mix twice as much Salt of Tartar <term>Salt of Tartar: potassium carbonate, a strong alkali</term> in a vial, and pour water over it to a depth of three or four fingers

Afterward, pour the entire matter into a tall **distillation flask** <note>original: Destillir-Kolben</note>, to which one attaches an **alembic** <note>original: Alembik; the cooling cap used in distillation</note> with a wax-plaster, and distill for several hours at a mild heat. Finally, draw off the **spirit of urine** <note>term: Urin-Geist; an alchemical term for ammonia-rich solution derived from fermented urine</note> from the remaining matter in a small distillation flask until it reaches an **oily essence**. Draw off the excess moisture from the spirit of urine, pour it back into that other remaining oily essence in the previous vial, and boil it anew gently for one or two days; draw it off again gradually, and do this for a third time. Then, save the spirit that has been drawn off; pour a sufficient quantity of moisture over the remaining **sediment** <note>original: Bodensatz</note> once more, if necessary, for several days, until it is **digested** <note>term: digerirt; a process of slow heating to promote chemical breakdown or maturation</note> at the required strong heat until it bubbles. Finally, one draws off the moisture until it reaches a thickness, lets the thick matter gradually and gently smoke off and dry, makes it smaller by rubbing <insert>grinding</insert>, and places the **sublimation** <note>original: Erhöhung; literally "elevation," referring to the process where a solid turns to vapor and collects at the top of the vessel</note> into a well-sealed distillation flask, where one increases the heat up to three or

# Fourth Section.

“ ...for four hours, gradually increasing the heat.
“ That which is elevated <note>sublimated or raised through heat</note> with two parts **Salt of Tartar** <term>Potassium carbonate, often used as a flux in alchemy</term> must be ground anew (either with or without moisture) and driven off from a **retort** <term>a glass vessel with a long downward-pointing neck used for distillation</term>. The neck of this retort—either its own or, what is better, an attached one—should reach slightly into the water placed within a **receiver** <term>a vessel designed to collect the products of distillation</term>. This should be done gradually over three or four hours with increased heat, though the fire should be dampened at the very end. In this manner, the **mercury** will be found in the receiving vessels. Thin **gold leaves** may be placed into the water so that the mercury, in the form of a vapor as thin as water held between them, may gradually flow together and be drawn closer and strengthened by them. From these, it can be drawn off by simple distillation and kept with the rest. The work can be completed more briefly by drawing off and **cohibating** <term>the process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from its own residue to increase its potency</term> the volatile spirit. In both methods, however, one must proceed slowly and deliberately during the elevation. By this method, three times the weight of the **regulus** <term>original: "Königs" - the pure metallic part of an ore that settles at the bottom during smelting</term> used was actually converted into mercury, and the experiment has demonstrated its own correctness.”

->§. 13.<-

Beginners in the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or early chemistry</note> may use this very same method, as they not infrequently despair of the success of their work when the black, gray, or iron-colored calx <note>A "calx" is the powdery metallic oxide remaining after a metal has been burned or roasted</note> refuses to take on a gold color in the fire, leading one to think it is a stubborn and useless material. # Of the Golden Fleece

Rhine gold, in contrast to Hungarian gold, has received its name.

Whether and how this Rhine gold, or paler gold, can be brought to the level of Hungarian gold in terms of quality has been much disputed among experts. They formerly called this art the Melandine Art (<term>Ars Melandina</term> <note>original: "Ars Melandina"; a historical term for the process of refining or "coloring" gold to improve its appearance and purity</note>). The question is not about any external color, such as one can give to it in coins, but rather about the internal quality.

There are many who promise to perform such a task through certain <term>cementations</term> <note>a chemical process where metals are heated with granulated materials to remove impurities</note>; but these are mostly "rare birds" <note>an idiom meaning people who are hard to find or perhaps untrustworthy</note> who can accomplish this without causing damage. For there is no doubt regarding the possibility of it.

I myself saw a solid gold ring, which contained an addition of silver, which remained pure gold without prejudice to its round form. I also saw golden Rhine coins which had been raised to the level of pure Hungarian gold, such that the inscription on the coin remained undamaged.

But whether this can also be done with profit, or at least without the loss of the silver mixed therein, or even of a portion of the gold, that is another question. Unless one understands the art of refining <note>original: "Zurücktreibungskunſt"; the skill of separating metals or driving off impurities</note> masterfully, he will...

->§. 15.<-

Whether Hungarian gold <note>original: "ungarisches Gold"; historically considered one of the purest forms of gold available in Europe</note>, or that which holds twenty-four carats <term>karat: a unit of purity for gold, where 24 carats represents pure gold</term>, can be further increased to twenty-eight or more carats—so that it raises any silver mixed with it to the status of gold, and thus finally returns to its previous state of twenty-four carats when combined with an equal portion of silver—is no less a question among the experts of the art.

For most assert this to be true, and wish to bring it to pass through the help of iron and copper, or their "sulfurs" <note>In alchemy, "sulfur" refers to the combustible or "soul" principle of a metal, not just the chemical element</note>, through repeated meltings. And this very point seems to agree principally with the thoughts of Beccher <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous German alchemist and physician</note> in his *Growth of Sand* <note>original: "Minera Arenaria"; a work concerning the extraction of gold from river sand</note>. And indeed, this is not without reason. For none among the philosophers doubt that the aforementioned metals possess an abundant, potent, golden sulfur, which, as it were, hastens toward effectiveness when supported by another active agent.

Thus writes the author of *Alchemy Unveiled* <note>original: "Denudatæ Alchymiæ"; likely referring to the work by Reibehand or a similar period text</note>: when the gold [is] twenty...

# Fourth Section.

### §. 17.

What else is the meaning of the many "graduating waters" <note>Gradierwasser: solutions used to "grade" or improve the quality and color of metals, often to make them appear more like gold</note> and the many "graduating vessels" which are encountered in countless numbers within alchemical processes? Regarding both paths—namely the wet and the dry—for introducing this so-called "sulfurous substance" <note>In 18th-century chemistry, "sulfur" often referred to the principle of combustibility and color, rather than the chemical element sulfur alone</note> into another metallic body that lacks this component, or where it is not present in sufficient quantity, the highly renowned Stahl <note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), a famous chemist and physician who developed the Phlogiston theory</note> speaks very beautifully in his *Foundations of Chemistry* <note>original: "Fundam. Chymiæ"</note> on page 150, §. 19, and so forth. There, as a master of the craft, he reminds us that one can intercept the volatile nature of this substance with clever and suitable vessels <note>original: "Gläsern," literally glasses or glass vessels</note> which, through their sticky nature, noticeably protect this delicate substance from the violence of the fire. One must only ensure that the flow <note>the molten state of the metal</note> must continue with these for several hours. In the same place, he also recommends a process or method via the wet path to make perfect metals "more than perfect" <note>A term used for increasing the density, color, or value of gold and silver</note>.

However, Kunkel <note>Johannes Kunckel (1630–1703), a famous German chemist and glassmaker</note> teaches the best graduating water—made from destroyed iron that has been purified in the form of a crystallized salt, and distilled with the vitriol of copper and with saltpeter—in his *Laboratory* in the 23rd chapter, page 345. ->§. 18.<-

That all experience is deceptive—even if Hippocrates <note>Referring to the famous aphorism "Ars longa, vita brevis, experientia fallax" (Art is long, life is short, experience deceptive)</note> had remained silent on the matter—is known to everyone. One reads much in the works of Boyle <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), a founding figure of modern chemistry</note> that is relevant to this.

For some things that were performed perfectly well the first time turn out poorly when they are repeated. Some things can only be performed on a small scale; both Kunckel <note>Johannes Kunckel, a famous 17th-century German chemist and glassmaker</note> and Glauber <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber, known for "Glauber's salt"</note> write much regarding this, especially in the preparations of mercuries <term>mercuries: in alchemy, this refers to the volatile or "spirit" essence of a metal, not just quicksilver</term>. Other things succeed better on a large scale, which yield little benefit in the small.

Becher <note>Johann Joachim Becher, the father of the Phlogiston theory</note> also writes of this in his *Sand-Growth* <note>original: "Minera Arenaria," a work concerning the extraction of gold from river sand</note>.

In a word, if the experiments are to have a good result, one must know everything precisely and in great detail, and observe during the work whatever is required in any way.

The experiment can serve us as an example which proves that in an empty and dead thing, no fire can be awakened. And this is entirely reliable, when everything is as it should be. Although Boyle and Schott <note>Gaspar Schott, a Jesuit physicist known for his work on hydraulics and mechanics</note>, in their
