# Fourth Section.

Certainly, great men attempt to write that they have experienced the opposite.

### §. 19.

Alongside the deceptive and uncertain nature of experiments, the many frauds of the alchemists also make the art vexing and unpleasant. Countless examples of these deceivers are brought to light here and there, against whom the wise men of old times have already commanded us to be on our guard.

A remarkable example is recounted by Beccher <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a famous German physician and alchemist known for his theory of "inflammable earth."</note> in the first part of his collection of Concordance <note>original: "in prima parte collectionis Concordantiæ suæ"</note>, which took place in Vienna. There, an artist of this sort made a contract with the Imperial Chamber President under seemingly honest conditions. The artist even staked his own gold against any potential loss should he fail, at least to the end that he might show the formula of this contract to Count Bucquoy—whom he knew to be a gentleman very eager for such arts—and thus lead him astray all the more easily.

Siberg's tricks, which he performed in a famous Imperial City only in our own times, are still known to everyone. <note>Siberg likely refers to a contemporary confidence man or "gold-maker" active in the late 17th or early 18th century.</note>

# Of the Golden Fleece

The deceptions, however, of Johann Ludwig Hannemann <note>A physician and professor at Kiel (1640–1724) who claimed to possess the Philosopher's Stone</note>, a doctor at Kiel, who sold worthless things under the guise of godliness, have been experienced by many men highly skilled in philosophy. If the stories arising from these events were to be made public, they would be deemed incredible. For who would have taken such a great adept and venerable elder—whose advice was like divine oracles to the great Morhof <note>Daniel Georg Morhof (1639–1691), a famous polymath and scholar who was apparently misled by Hannemann</note>—for a fraud, especially since no dire necessity forced him into it?

Also throughout all of Germany in our time, that masked Naragoras <note>A pseudonym likely referring to a specific contemporary swindler, derived from "Narr" (fool) and "Agoras" (assembly/market)</note> has become well-known and very famous under various names. He knew how to masterfully deprive both princes and private individuals of their money, so that they could not feel the deception or the inflicted wound until the blood had already flowed.

The stories of such fraudsters known only to us would truly fill a large book, if we wished to touch upon them briefly as is proper. However, the limits of a polemical treatise <note>original: "Streitſchrift"</note>, by which we are bound, do not permit this. Yet, more remarkable things may be read in the *Chemical Robinson* <note>original: "Robinſono Chemico," a satirical work regarding alchemical adventures</note>, which was recently published by Hydrophilus.

# Fourth Section.

### §. 20.

It is a generally known fact that the so-called <term>Aquafort</term> <note>Latin: aqua fortis, or "strong water," known today as nitric acid</note> or <term>Scheidwasser</term> <note>Literally "parting water," used to separate gold from silver</note>, which chemists employ as necessary instruments for the dissolution, separation, or examination of metals, is distilled from saltpeter, vitriol, or alum.

However, all those who use saltpeter that has not been sufficiently purified from common salt deceive others and are themselves deceived. For in such a case, they distill a water that is unfit for the separation of gold and silver, because the spirit of salt <note>hydrochloric acid</note>—along with the saltpeter—attacks the gold itself.

Therefore, Beccher <note>Johann Joachim Beccher (1635–1682), a famous German alchemist and physician</note> rightly complains in his work *The Sand-Growth* (in <term>Minera Arenaria</term> <note>Latin: "Sand Ore"</note>) about the malice of assayers who use such parting water, unpurified of common salt, for the testing of their ores. And through this deception, it has happened that less gold was precipitated than had been claimed or indicated.

Just as one obtains less gold in this manner, it also occasionally happens that there is vitriol impregnated with "gold-seed"; when this is distilled with saltpeter, one believes it yields a common parting—

# Of the Golden Fleece

Nitric Acid <note>original: "Scheidwaſſer" – literally "parting water," the historical term for nitric acid used to separate gold from silver</note>. However, when it is used in the art of assaying and refining to separate gold, it often leaves behind as much gold-calx <note>a metallic powder or oxide remaining after heating or chemical treatment</note> as one would never obtain through another nitric acid. In such a manner, no small deceptions can occur.

We have an example recounted in the *Defense of Alchemy* <note>original: "Vindiciis Alchymiæ," likely referring to a Latin treatise defending the legitimacy of transmutation</note> concerning an assayer from Vienna who was deeply in debt. When he had been reduced to the beggar's staff, he distilled ten hundredweight of Hungarian Vitriol <note>iron or copper sulfate</note> with saltpeter in the common way to make a nitric acid; from the dissolved silver, he obtained more than ten thousand ducats of the finest gold. And this would never have been possible with any other vitriol. Without doubt, the volatile gold in such ores united itself with the silver in its innermost being through the stabilized spirit of saltpeter, and Phoebus <note>The sun, representing gold in alchemical allegory</note> made the particle of his sister <note>The moon, representing silver</note> like unto himself, and ripened it in his own fire.

-> §. 21. <-

Therefore, I believe that the question which has not yet been cleared up by the assayers can be explained without difficulty—the question which they, to use their own expressions, call the

# Fourth Section.

I 5

Bring forward the ambush of the <term>parting acid</term> <note>original: "Scheidwasser"; usually nitric acid used to separate gold from silver</note>; when during the assay and separation the gold is not diminished, but rather increased.

The question is whether this increase of the black <term>calx</term> <note>original: "Kalk"; the powdery residue left after heating or chemical reaction</note>, which has settled on the bottom of the vessel and retained its luster in the furnace along with the rest of the gold, is true gold? Or is it merely fragments of silver clinging to the gold, hidden under the color of the larger parts, which have given the gold-calx a greater weight than it previously had? Also, those do not seem to be in the wrong who hold with <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a famous German alchemist and physician</note> Becher that the constituent part of sulfur lies hidden within the <term>spirit of nitre</term> <note>original: "Salpetergeist"; nitric acid</note> under a black color, and eagerly takes on the matter of the gold (which is not yet sufficiently formed) in place of a form. That is to say: the volatile makes its own nature mature, as much as the conditions of time and the more or less similar matter allow.

->§. 22.<-

Even in ancient times, the deceptions of goldsmiths who mixed silver into gold were well known. This was discovered by <term>hydrostatics</term> <note>The science of fluids at rest and the pressure they exert</note>, which Archimedes invented. Even in our own times, there is no shortage of those who—

# Of the Golden Fleece

who have dared to play such deceptions with golden vessels and rings. For it is an easy art to create such a metallic mixture that resembles true gold so closely that it can withstand the test of the touchstone <note>A dark stone used to test the purity of precious metals by the color of the streak they leave behind.</note> and of the parting-water <note>Nitric acid, used to separate gold from silver.</note>. For they know how to mix gold, silver, and copper together in a certain proportion, and by adding certain salts—namely vitriol <note>Sulfate minerals</note>, saltpeter, and sal ammoniac <note>Ammonium chloride</note>—they precipitate the gold onto the surface of the mass in such a way that, by the aforementioned tests, one would take it entirely for true gold. And to this fraud, Schröder has given the right name: the Rogue’s Tincture or Art. For in this manner, all those can be deceived who are satisfied with these common tests and do not know, or shy away from, that rigorous Lycaonian test <note>original: "Lykaonische Probe"; likely a reference to a more severe or "cruel" fire-test, named after the mythological King Lycaon</note>, which opportunity or other circumstances often do not allow one to perform.

->§. 23.<-

For it is not difficult, as the author of *Alchemy Unveiled* <note>original: "denudatæ Alchymiæ," likely referring to the work by Johann Joachim Becher or a similar period text</note> says, to bring silver to such a state in a short time with the help of sulfur that it remains untouched by <term>parting water<note>Nitric acid, used to separate gold from silver</note></term>.

I have performed these experiments, but did not find them correct in all parts. Previously, when I was not yet sufficiently well-versed in the "Kitchen of <term>Geber<note>An 8th-century Arabic alchemist; his "kitchen" refers to the practical laboratory setting</note></term>," I allowed myself to be persuaded by such easy labor—namely, by <term>cementing<note>A process of heating metals with powdered reagents</note></term> and melting with antimony, etc.—that silver could be made so stable that, when united with gold, it would withstand every test. Similarly, I remember reading in a French author that gold which has been cemented seven times with the help of copper and salts—taking only the tincture <note>the concentrated essence or color-giving principle</note>—becomes so heightened in color that no one would take it for gold. And experience confirms this as well. This gold, when combined with an equal weight of silver, will give the silver the color of gold.

But I advise that you do not do this, because through frequent melting in the fire, it might lose its color and become white once again.

# Of the Golden Fleece

### §. 25.

It is certain that a tincture <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is a concentrated substance or liquid capable of imparting its color or properties to another body.</note> can be extracted from iron and copper with little effort and communicated to lead during melting, so that it becomes colored with a permanent gold color both inside and out. This golden lead can be beaten into leaves just like gold itself, and other things—whether wood or metals—can be gilded with it as if with leaf gold.

In the meantime, the proverb applies here: "All that glitters is not gold." No honest man would use such tricks to deceive anyone. I do not know whether that metal which resembles gold—and which is available today in France from Monsieur de Renti with the approval of the Royal Academy of Sciences, as is known from the public newspapers—is of this kind? Or whether it is such a red or brown <term>bronze: a metal alloy</term> metal, which was already prepared by German artists in Nuremberg in the previous century so that it could be hammered and drawn; we spoke of this above at the end of the second section.

# Fourth Section.

### §. 26.

We wish to speak briefly regarding several supposed transformations of metals, which are boasted of by those who have not yet looked deeply enough into the nature of things.

Among these is the transformation of copper from iron, from which Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> and others promise much.

However, such processes, as many as I have read of them, are nothing other than the precipitation of copper, which has been made from a solution of vitriol <note>term: Vitriol (a historical term for sulfate minerals, here likely copper sulfate or "blue vitriol")</note> by means of iron.

I have tested some of these myself, observing how in the Hungarian ore mines, iron sheets—when placed in vitriol-water—are eaten away, and in place of the iron, copper particles are accumulated, visibly retaining the exact shape of the sheet, for example, that of a horseshoe. I wondered, therefore, that the very ingenious Stahl <note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), a chemist famous for the phlogiston theory.</note> expressly writes in his *Foundations of Chemistry* <note>original: "Fundamentis Chymiæ"</note>: "That true copper comes from iron is attested by general experience."

It is indeed correct that there is hardly iron anywhere that is entirely without copper, which is easily extracted by spirit of urine <note>term: Spirit of urine (an aqueous solution of ammonia, used as a chemical reagent).</note>.

<summary>The author critiques the alchemical belief that iron can be transformed into copper, explaining that such phenomena are actually chemical precipitations or the extraction of existing impurities rather than true transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>transmutation, alchemy, copper, iron, vitriol, Paracelsus, Georg Ernst Stahl, chemical precipitation, mining history</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's skeptical examination of alchemical transmutations, specifically addressing the apparent transformation of base metals into precious ones through chemical reactions.</meta>

# Of the Golden Fleece

And through this sign and appearance, a famous physician—who is renowned for his sky-blue iron tincture <note>likely a reference to a specific chemical preparation of iron sulfate or a similar compound used in early medicine</note>—has deceived both himself and others.

However, whether this can be called a transformation of iron into copper (taken in the strictest sense), I doubt. With no better reasoning, some believed that cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note> transformed itself into silver, since it is customary to place both layer upon layer in the crucible; instead of the "nails" of the cinnabar, silver particles are gathered together, resembling a certain blackish silver ore commonly called glass-ore <note>acanthite or silver glance</note>.

And we have performed this experiment according to Schröder's instructions <note>referring to Johann Schröder, a prominent 17th-century pharmacologist</note> more than once. When we weighed it precisely, we found that as much was lost from the remaining silver-calx <note>an oxide or powder resulting from heating the metal</note> as the silver had gained through the pieces of cinnabar by the effect and power of the dissolving and blackening sulfuric acid <note>original: "Schwefel-Sauren"</note> addition.

But whether this work is truly worthy of philosophical investigation, we gladly and willingly leave to Baron Schröder.

# Fourth Section.

### §. 28.

The transformation of lead into silver is no less worthy of a precise investigation. It appears that Kunckel <note>Johannes Kunckel (c. 1630–1703), a famous German chemist and glassmaker who served at the court of Frederick William of Prussia.</note> himself believed in this.

When lead is melted with horn silver <note>original: "Hornsilber"; a historical term for silver chloride (chlorargyrite).</note>, the lead absorbs exactly as much from the silver and yields it back again on the cupel <note>original: "Kapell"; a shallow, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead.</note> as there was silver at the beginning. Anyone will find this to be quite correct.

However, the driving back of the slag <note>original: "Schlaken"; the stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting.</note> is not advised in Kunckel's opinion. We would therefore rather set down the opinion of Tachenius <note>Otto Tachenius (c. 1610–1680), a German chemist and physician known for his work on the composition of salts.</note> regarding this work than our own, which is not yet sufficiently established. This famous man writes in the ninth chapter of *The Key to Hippocratic Medicine* <note>original: "Clave Medicinæ Hippocraticæ", published in 1666.</note> as follows: Let there be one ounce of silver dissolved by aqua fortis <note>original: "Scheidwasser"; literally "parting water," the historical term for nitric acid used to separate gold and silver.</note>. Evaporate this solution until it becomes a salty powder; cast it upon lead that has been melted in the fire, but which is neither too hot nor entirely cold. In the space of a quarter of an hour, the acidic salt of the aqua fortis leaves the silver and corrodes as much lead as it had previously "spoiled" silver—namely, one ounce. The remaining part of the lead, because it performs the office of a metallic alkali <note>term: metallic alkali (a historical chemical concept where certain metals or substances were thought to act as bases to neutralize acids)</note>, attracts and saturates itself with the silver, which

### §. 29.

Most learned men have marveled at this <term>mechanics<note>In this context, the physical or chemical process of the reaction.</note></term>. For they extracted an ounce of silver from the lead, and found the powder in exactly the same form and weight as they had thrown it in. They were therefore of the opinion that it was the very same powder of silver which they had thrown upon it. And this would provide them with a very great profit, if only a means could be found to melt the powder back into a solid body. But just as the magnet attracts iron, so also does the lead (acting as an <term>alkali<note>Here used in the archaic sense of a neutralizing agent or a base in the "Art" of alchemy.</note></term> in the Art) attract the silver to itself. And since the thirsty salt of the <term>aqua fortis<note>original: "Scheidwasser" - literally "parting water," usually nitric acid used to separate gold from silver.</note></term>, which has no watery moisture, cannot exist alone, it attracts as much lead to itself as it has destroyed of the silver.

To this experiment, says <note>Francesco Lana de Terzi (1631–1687), an Italian Jesuit and naturalist.</note> Father Francesco Terzi de Lana, the one is similar which he recounted in his <note>original: "Prodromo" - referring to his 1670 work *Prodromo ovvero saggio di alcune invenzioni nuove*.</note> preface regarding tin; which, when it is placed into small chalk-balls and thrown into the open silver, changes itself into true silver. And nonetheless, just as much tin is afterwards found in the melted lead itself.

...then previously having taken upon himself a pure and perfect body.

Godly souls, when considering the necessity of this <term>corporealization</term> <note>German: Leiblichmachen; the process of a spirit or energy taking on a physical form</note>, can make the most glorious reflections on the perfect imagery of the revelation of the Son of God in the flesh, so that he might restore the salvation of humanity. The Word became flesh so that what is divine power could communicate itself by means of human nature and cleanse it from original sin.

### §. 34.

It happens not infrequently, and especially during <term>particular transformations</term> <note>Alchemical operations intended to transmute only a portion of a base metal into gold or silver, rather than a total transformation</note>, that the <term>tincture</term> <note>The potent liquid or powder used to catalyze transmutation</note> does not bring the entire substance of the imperfect metal into the nature of gold or silver. Instead, after pouring it into the <term>ingot mold</term> <note>German: Innguß</note>, the matter acquires a certain king-like, brittle, and crumbly mass that is fit neither for drawing into wire nor for hammering. In such cases, the resistance of the parts still mixed within prevents it from becoming soft or ductile, so that the inexperienced consider it a useless metal.

We have examples of this kind in the *Vindication of Alchemy* <note>original: "Vindiciis Alchymiæ"; likely referring to a specific defense of the art against its critics</note>.

When the slag has already been separated from it, the "king" <note>The refined metallic mass.</note> is washed from the lead on the cupel <note><term>Cupel: a shallow, porous dish used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead and other impurities.</term></note>, where then at last it becomes manifest

# Fourth Section.

it becomes clear how far the powers of the tincture <note>The "tincture" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the transformative substance used in alchemy to transmute metals.</note> extend.

In the writings of Helvetius <note>Johann Friedrich Helvetius (1630–1709), a famous physician and alchemist known for his reported successful transmutation in 1666.</note> we read an example of gold made through art, which, even in the assaying furnace, transformed a certain quantity of lead; thus, the lead can always determine the correct dosage of the tincture and the imperfect metal.

---

### §. 35.

To be sure, many obstacles can occur in these works for the inexperienced. There are, moreover, several other artistic tricks and advantages that have their uses, but to cite them all would exceed the limits of an academic treatise, which are too narrow.

We wish to append only one more thing for the sake of those who can indeed impregnate silver with some gold from all kinds of ore-growths—whether from sulfurous, metallic, or graduation waters <note>Graduation waters (Gradierwasser) were saline solutions used in the process of refining salts or metals through evaporation.</note>—but do not know how to separate a small quantity of gold from a larger amount of silver without suffering a loss.

For whether one chooses to use aqua regia <note>original: "Königswasser"; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acid used to dissolve gold.</note> or nitric acid <note>original: "Scheidwasser"; literally "parting water," used to separate silver from gold.</note> for assistance, the costs will always exceed the profit.

# Of the Golden Fleece

For example, if there is half a <term>drachm</term><note>A small unit of weight, roughly 3.7 grams.</note> of gold in an entire half-pound of silver, a whole pound of <term>parting acid</term><note>Nitric acid, used to separate gold from silver.</note> can easily be consumed for its separation. Furthermore, if one calculates the time, labor, vessels, and coals, and ignores the loss of silver, then the costs spent on such arts are indeed pleasant to a philosopher—who views wealth as something trivial merely for the love of truth—but truly harmful to one hungry for gold. They will fulfill that proverb: *When labor results in loss, mortal want increases.* <note>original: "Cum Labor in damno est crescit mortalis egestas."</note>

In the Saxon manuscripts and in the writings of Kunkel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703), a famous chemist and glassmaker.</note> one reads: "that they initially refined such <insert>artificially made</insert> gold in the mint; however, this seemed to take too long for the Elector August <note>August I, Elector of Saxony (1526–1586), known for his interest in alchemy and technology.</note>, who wrote with his own hand: 'The <term>cement-parting</term><note>A dry refining process using salts and heat to purify gold.</note> pleases me well enough, but I must always wait over three days for my gold, and that is too long for me (for at one time there were nearly two hundred <term>marks</term><note>A unit of weight for precious metals, roughly 233 grams.</note> in the cement, and otherwise he used to say: I fear a long duration as I fear the Devil); I stick with <term>water-parting</term><note>Separation using liquid acids.</note>.' This, however, also did not want to suffice because of the large quantity."

# Fourth Section.

> "... finally, it was decided that matters should be left as they were, and only one part should be refined so that the alloying <note>original: "Beschickung" — the process of preparing a metal mixture for smelting</note> could be arranged accordingly. And from this came the Rhenish gold guilders of the Electorate of Saxony, of which Kunkel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703), a famous German chemist and glassmaker</note> writes: 'Whoever possesses such coins, I can assure them on my conscience that the gold within them is produced through the Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy or advanced chemical transmutation</note>.'"

However, these inconveniences are remedied by the new invention of dry separation in fire. This method—which is known to everyone even without the purification in "King’s Cement" <note>original: "Königs-Cement" — a dry refining process using salts to purify gold</note>—teaches how to precipitate the heavier metal, namely gold, from the lighter ones during the melt to the bottom of the crucible. It brings the gold into the center so that it can be separated from the upper mass (which contains the remaining silver) with a single blow of the hammer. If any of the silver has precipitated along with it, it can be freed from it using a much smaller quantity of parting water <note>original: "Scheidwasser" — nitric acid used to separate gold from silver</note>. This technical feat is called "separation by casting and fluxing" <note>original: "Scheiden im Guß und Fluß"</note>, and it is not sufficiently known to all assayers and goldsmiths.

The incomparable Borrichius <note>Olaus Borrichius (1626–1690), a Danish polymath and chemist</note> and Ercker <note>Lazarus Ercker (c. 1530–1594), author of a famous treatise on ores and assaying</note> have already made this work known to the public. For the former writes briefly as follows:

# Of the Golden Fleece

In order that the gold may be driven into a narrow space—that is, to flow together into one place—Ercker <note>Lazarus Ercker (c. 1530–1594), a famous Bohemian metallurgist and author of "Description of Leading Ore-processing and Mining Methods."</note> proposes a beautiful, short method which others also wish to achieve in this way: They take arsenic and sulfur, as much as desired (equal parts of each), rub and mix them together, and immediately pour half an ounce of this powder into half a pound of molten silver. They apply a strong fire so that the mixture flows well together and the heavier material settles at the bottom. Finally, at the bottom of the crucible—once it is removed and cooled—they find a <term>Regulus</term> <note>original: "König" (King). In alchemy and metallurgy, this refers to the metallic button or mass found at the bottom of a crucible after smelting.</note> which is rich in gold and can be purified with little effort. So far writes Borrichius <note>Olaus Borrichius (1626–1690), a Danish polymath known for his works on chemistry and hermetic philology.</note>.

Some take sulfur, tartar <note>Potassium bitartrate, often used as a flux in smelting.</note>, and antimony <note>original: "Spießglas" (Stibnite/Antimony sulfide).</note>. Others add salt to the antimony and sulfur. Some make various other mixtures. However, the silver which remains hidden after the melting beneath these substances—which lie on top of the gold and are called by the technical term <term>Blachmal</term> <note>A metallurgical term for the dross or "scoria" consisting of a mixture of lead, sulfur, and other minerals that forms during the separation of gold and silver.</note>—is recovered when one melts it with lead and treats it on the cupel <note>original: "Kapelle." A shallow, porous cup used in cupellation to separate precious metals from base metals like lead.</note> according to its nature.

But so that beginners may have some theory regarding this magnificent invention and may thoroughly understand the process of how it is to be done, they should know that it depends solely and only

# Of the Golden Fleece

Regarding the **Spagyric Art** <note>The art of alchemy, specifically the process of separating and recombining the components of substances to achieve a higher state.</note>, I can only sincerely recommend at the end that you do not believe it to be as easy as it may appear to the inexperienced; and that you do not set your hand to the work unless you first thoroughly understand what the **Philosophers** <note>A term used by alchemists to refer to their predecessors and masters of the "Hermetic Philosophy."</note> mean in their writings by the word **"Ours."**

For when they make use of common names and well-known designations for things, let this always remind you: they wish to have nothing else understood by these names than that which is **"Ours."** Pontanus <note>likely Joannes Pontanus, an alchemist famous for his "Epistle" concerning the "Philosophical Fire."</note>, a famous writer, testifies of himself that although he worked with the true matter, he nevertheless erred very often because he did not understand **Our Fire**.

Antonius de Abbatia <note>A 14th-century Italian alchemist known for his letters on the transmutation of metals.</note> writes very sincerely the following to the Brothers: "Take care, my beloved, that our words do not lead you astray, and that you do not spend your time uselessly with new hopes. Just as it once happened to a certain artist who had read and heard our letter stating that without **Our Water** nothing can come to be; he then remembered that we called it *Aqua Vitae* <note>Latin for "Water of Life," usually referring to concentrated alcohol or a potent solvent.</note>. But he was ultimately deceived and compelled to seek something else, and to read our letter once more, and he found that our water also..."

<summary>This page warns aspiring alchemists that common terms like "water" or "fire" in hermetic texts refer to secret, proprietary substances ("Ours") rather than everyday materials. It cites the failures of famous figures like Pontanus to emphasize the necessity of decoding this philosophical language.</summary>
<keywords>Spagyric Art, Alchemy, Alchemical Fire, Alchemical Water, Pontanus, Antonius de Abbatia, Aqua Vitae, Hermetic Philosophy</keywords>

**Raymond Lully**<note>A major 13th-century philosopher and alchemist</note>, the teacher of all teachers, says: We call it "sealed earth," our elevated bodies placed upon a high stage, our sulfur, our mercury, our arsenic, wherein you must obtain our gold, which is our ferment and our <term>living lime</term><note>Quicklime or a caustic philosophical base</note>, and which begets within itself a "son of fire," who is the beloved of the philosophers.

**Bernard, the Count of Trevisan**<note>A famous 15th-century alchemist known for his long search for the Stone</note>, commands us to despise all those who say there is any sulfur other than ours, any mercury other than ours—which is found in the <term>magnesia</term><note>Not modern magnesium, but a mysterious "mother" substance of the Stone</note>—or any vinegar other than ours. For there is no other sulfur, no other mercury, and no other vinegar than ours.

**Efferarius**, a monk, writes to a certain Apostolic official: Let him be silent who says there is another gold besides ours, another water besides ours—which is also called the "sourest vinegar"—another dissolution and coagulation besides ours—which occurs with a gentle fire—another putrefaction besides ours, or another volatile thing or another spirit besides ours, namely

### Third Section.

namely mercury and sulfur, another alum or salt according to our own, which, when it is composed in the whiteness, is called the flower of white salt, or another egg <note>original: "Ay" (Ei); a common alchemical metaphor for the vessel or the primordial matter</note> according to our own, or another human blood according to our own, and so forth.

So also says Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist</note>, or for all I care Setonius <note>Alexander Sethon, the "Cosmopolite," often confused with his student Sendivogius</note> in his treatise on sulfur <note>original: "in Tractatu de sulphure"</note>: "Hear what a difference there is between our mercury and common mercury," etc. And again Lullius <note>Ramon Llull, a Majorcan philosopher to whom many alchemical works were attributed</note>: "Common mercury, let it be prepared however it will, cannot be our mercury." And yet all philosophers cry out with one voice that they do not mean common things, but our things, whether they be produced by nature or art; and they remind the seekers <note>original: "Liebhaber" (lovers/amateurs of the art)</note> that they should not let themselves be deceived by the various names, so that no one can complain about them as if they had intentionally wanted to lead beginners astray, which is not fitting for an honorable man.

So much for this time!

# Additions.
### Regarding the 1st, 2nd, and 3rd Sections.

---

1.) Alchemy is a daughter of Wisdom, of which the *Book of Proverbs* <note>original: "Sepher Misle" (Hebrew: Sefer Mishlei)</note> says in the 16th verse of the third chapter, according to the German translation: "Length of days is in her right hand, and in her left hand are riches and honor. Her ways are ways of pleasantness, and all her paths are peace. She is a tree of life to all who lay hold upon her, and happy are those who retain her."

2.) It matters little whether one writes with the Arabic article <note>The prefix "Al-"</note> *Alchemy*, or without it, *Chymistry*, or *Chimia*, *Chemia*, or whether one says *Halchymia*, or *Spagyrics* <term>Spagyrica: a term coined by Paracelsus referring to the art of separating and recombining natural substances</term>, and so forth. All these names mean the same thing to us.

3.) Thus, Alchemy and Natural Philosophy <note>original: "Weltweißheit," literally "world-wisdom," a common 18th-century term for science and philosophy</note>, which deals with experiments, are either not distinguished from one another at all, or the former is merely a specific part of the latter.

4.) For where body and spirit are removed from their natural state, divided, and recombined, they are thereby made fit for various uses.

<summary>This page defines alchemy as a divine pursuit synonymous with wisdom and natural philosophy, emphasizing that the various names for the craft all describe the same experimental science of transforming matter.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Wisdom, Book of Proverbs, Spagyrics, Natural Philosophy, Body and Spirit, Etymology</keywords>

# Additions.

5.) One can also divide [the subject] in two ways: either in regard to the purpose or the object; either regarding the works themselves, or in another consideration. And from this arise the various designations.

6.) Nature itself always plays the role of a tireless and very wise chemist <term>chemist: here referring to the alchemical tradition of transforming matter</term> by destroying, procreating, separating, combining, dissolving, coagulating, extracting, purifying, remixing, refining, thickening, elevating, precipitating, distilling, restraining <note>original: "Cohibiren," a term for preventing the escape of volatile spirits</note>, soaking, drying out, incinerating, stabilizing, boiling, ripening, and returning things to a raw state, and so forth.

7.) Now, Art <note>Art here refers to human skill or technology, specifically alchemy</note> imitates Nature, but also goes further.

8.) Putrefaction and incineration are the primary means of transformation in both Nature and Art.

9.) One learns this best from the Author of Nature. Therefore, all human science is deficient and imperfect without a true knowledge of God.

10.) Experience—the teacher of the wise, not of fools—testifies that in all things there is an active and a passive power.

11.) From the action and passion <note>the state of being acted upon</note> of two or more materials, the change and transformation of things arises.

19.) One can make the journey to Colchis <note>original: "Colchus"; a reference to the destination of the Argonauts in Greek mythology, often used by alchemists as a metaphor for the quest for the Philosopher's Stone</note> in four different ways. 1.) By flight, through the air; 2.) by ship, through the water; 3.) on foot, through the earth; 4.) on horseback, upon a beast.

20.) Hermes was called the "Thrice Great" <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the origin of alchemy and hermetic philosophy</note> because he knew the three parts of worldly wisdom; although, according to the teachings of Suidas <note>A 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia</note>, he also spoke of the Trinity.

21.) The three parts of worldly wisdom <term>Weltweisheit: philosophy or natural science</term> are called the knowledge of the three kingdoms: the Mineral, Vegetable, and Animal kingdoms.

22.) These three kingdoms are indeed sufficient, yet one can also speak of four, and add to the preceding ones a fourth: namely, the Kingdom of Meteors <note>In early modern science, "meteors" referred to all atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and lightning</note>.

23.) The air is the temple wherein the matrimonial unions of the Heavens and the Earth are solemnized.

24.) The author of the *New Chemical Light* <note>original: "Novi Luminis Chymici"; a famous alchemical treatise published in 1604 by Michael Sendivogius</note> rightly asserts that there is a hidden food of life <note>original: "Lebens-Speisse"; likely referring to the "vital air" or oxygen-like principle sought by early chemists</note> within the air.

25.) The compression of the air has not yet been sufficiently researched, and it yields admirable signs.

<meta>This page continues the numbered list of scientific and alchemical axioms, following the previous page's discussion on the properties of matter.</meta>

# Additions.

26.) The more air is compressed, the more force it acquires; nevertheless, it remains transparent and does not turn into water.

27.) Whoever can thicken the air so that it can neither rise when left to itself nor when exposed to fire, will be able to create crystals, gemstones, diamonds, and more, according to the *New Chemical Light* <note>original: "Novum Lumen Chymicum," a highly influential alchemical text published in 1604 by Michael Sendivogius</note>.

28.) The thicker a body is made (including air itself), the heavier it becomes.

29.) Thickness <note>In this context, "thickness" refers to density or opacity</note> does not hinder transparency.

30.) No body is without pores <note>original: "Schweißlöcher," literally "sweat-holes," a common 17th-century term for microscopic pores in matter</note>.

31.) In a single body, the pores can be of various sizes and shapes.

32.) The difference in weight provides not the slightest proof for the existence of a vacuum <note>original: "das Leere," referring to the "void," a major point of debate in early modern physics</note>.

33.) In a single thing, weight and volatility, stability and subtlety <note>term: Subtlety (the quality of being refined, thin, or highly penetrative matter)</note> can exist to the highest degree.

34.) From the stable, one can make something volatile; from the thick, something thin; from the heavy, something light. And conversely: from the light, something heavy; from the thin, something thick; and from the volatile, something stable.

<summary>This page lists alchemical and physical axioms (numbers 26-34) concerning the compressibility of air, the nature of density and transparency, and the possibility of transmuting the physical states of matter.</summary>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered list of aphorisms or observations regarding alchemy and natural philosophy, following the catchword "43.) That" from the previous page.</meta>

# Additions

35.) Gold can be made so subtle <note>refined or volatile</note> that it burns with brandy <note>distilled alcohol</note> and vanishes entirely into the air, even though in its natural composition it laughs at all the power of Vulcan <note>the Roman god of fire; here meaning the heat of the furnace</note> for all eternity.

36.) Gold allows itself to be stretched so extensively that a single grain can be divided with small shears into 100,000 parts, each appearing as large as the original grain itself.

37.) Gold expands even further during the process of glass-staining when it has been precipitated with tin, as the experiments of Kunckel <note>Johannes Kunckel (c. 1630–1703), a famous German chemist and glassmaker known for his work on ruby glass</note> demonstrate.

38.) The processes of the philosophers <note>alchemists</note> are mostly false, and yet these same processes are also truthful.

39.) A philosopher has no need for the processes of others; instead, he creates his own processes as he desires.

40.) One never conducts an experiment without benefit. For the one that fails is often more instructive than the one that succeeds.

41.) Those who, in their labors, have no aim other than gold and silver are not worthy of the name of philosophers.

42.) To a philosopher, the transformation of gold into iron—or of a noble metal into a base one—is more important than the transformation of a base metal into a noble one.

<summary>This page lists several alchemical paradoxes and philosophical maxims, emphasizing the physical properties of gold and the ethical mindset required of a true natural philosopher.</summary>

# ->Additions<-

43.) That iron is more necessary to man than gold is clearly proven by the fact that the former grows abundantly everywhere, while the latter is found very little.

44.) One reads an excellent passage in the fourth book of Ezra <note>Also known as 2 Esdras in some English Bibles; a popular apocryphal work in the 18th century</note> in the 2nd verse of the 8th chapter, where the angel Uriel answers: If you ask the earth, it will tell you that it yields much soil from which earthen vessels are made, but only a little dust from which gold comes.

45.) We maintain that there are "iron-hard" people, not only in a moral and metaphorical sense, but also in a physical and literal sense.

46.) Those who deny the influence of the stars <note>original: "Gestirns"; referring to the astrological or physical influence of celestial bodies</note> know nothing of light in their pitch-black darkness, and do not understand the connection and the coherence of the system of the world.

47.) That the stars have their inhabitants, who are suited to the nature of each, has more probability than that such great bodies should be empty. Whether the passage in Nehemiah in the 6th verse of the 9th chapter can be explained in this sense, we shall save for investigation. <note>Nehemiah 9:6 refers to the "host of heaven" worshipping God, which the author interprets as potential extraterrestrial life.</note>

48.) Stones with figures <note>likely referring to fossils or figured stones</note> do not prove the universality of the Great Flood.

49.) If the diligence and experience of Glauber <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber (1604–1670), a famous apothecary and chemist known for "Glauber's salt."</note>, the ingenuity and erudition of Becher <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a physician and scholar who laid the foundations for the phlogiston theory.</note>, the luck and skill of Kunckel <note>Johannes Kunckel (1630–1703), a celebrated chemist and glassmaker who discovered phosphorus.</note>, along with the fear of God, had all come together in one person, they would have made the most excellent chemist.

50. Conrad Horn, formerly a very famous professor at the high school in Jülich, says rightly in the short summary of natural philosophy in the 26th sentence of the 10th disputation: <note>original: "in Compendio Philos. Natur. Disp. x. Tom. 26"</note>

-> If nature can transmute metals (which daily experience teaches the miners happens in the ore mines), then art can also do this, namely when it connects the active and the passive with one another. This is nothing absurd, nothing impossible: it can therefore happen! <-

# Additions.

2.) We can encounter very grievous and disadvantageous things, such as: trembling of the limbs, loss of teeth, headaches, heavy burdens <note>likely referring to a sense of oppression or respiratory distress</note>, gout, severe fainting spells, suffocation, blindness, sudden loss of voice, coughing, violent catarrhs <note>excessive buildup of mucus</note>, consumption <note>tuberculosis</note>, lung disease, lethargy and weakness of the limbs, ulcers, violent bleeding, or thickening of the blood as the cause of many diseases, etc. Of these, there are examples well-known to everyone today: α. The invention of gunpowder from a mixture of saltpeter, sulfur, tartar, or charcoal. β. Fulminating gold <note>original: "Schlaggold"; an explosive gold compound</note>, which is precipitated with salt of tartar, and silver, which has been made with spirit of urine <note>ammonia</note> through quicklime. γ. The sublimation <note>original: "Erhöhung"; literally 'elevation' or 'raising'</note> of gold, mercury, arsenic, and other minerals and spirits. δ. The fire-breathing dragon, of which was spoken in the introduction, etc.

For which reason the lovers of chemistry <note>term: "Chemistry" (Chemie); in this 18th-century context, it refers to both practical chemistry and speculative alchemy</note> are to be reminded in conclusion that they diligently note the advice which Medea gave to Jason when he had to plow with fire-breathing oxen: namely, that he should not drive with his plow against the wind, so that the violence of the flame might not seize him.

# Additions.

2.) We cannot doubt that God was able, from the beginning, to create all things exactly as they truly exist in their current composition.

3.) A true philosopher <note>In this context, "philosopher" refers to a natural scientist or alchemist seeking universal truths.</note> concerns himself little with all sorts of hypotheses; instead, he contemplates this created universe, its parts, and the smaller particles of those parts, just as he finds them. He observes and examines them with his senses and reason, and proceeds in this analytical order as far as it is possible for him.

4.) The chemistry of the philosophers <term>Alchemy: the "true chemistry" aimed at understanding the spiritual and material essence of matter</term> is the true key through which the secrets of nature may be opened. For even Jupiter, without the help of Vulcan <note>In Roman mythology, Vulcan (the god of fire/smithing) split Jupiter's skull with an axe to release Minerva (goddess of wisdom). This serves as an allegory for the necessity of "fire" and "labor" in attaining wisdom.</note>, could not conceive and give birth to Minerva from his brain.

5.) A philosopher who is simultaneously a Christian recognizes with a most grateful heart what the Author of Nature Himself wished to reveal concerning natural things in the Holy Scriptures, so that we rightly regard these as the primary object of philosophical knowledge.

6.) The preparation of the Philosopher's Stone <term>Lapis Philosophorum: the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting enlightenment</term>, and its primary matter, is depicted in the First Book of Moses <note>The Book of Genesis</note> in the second chapter, in verses 7, 21, 22, 23, and 24. <note>These verses describe the creation of Adam from the dust and the formation of Eve from Adam's rib, interpreted here as an alchemical allegory for the separation and union of elements.</note>

9.) The human arts take their beginning when they imitate the divine art, namely that of Nature.

10.) We believe without any doubt that there exist several tinctures <note>concentrated medicinal or alchemical substances</note> which are useful both for medicine and for the transmutation of metals.

22.) The opinion of Ozanam <note>Jacques Ozanam (1640–1717), a French mathematician</note> is quite absurd, claiming that the fixed stars borrow their light from our sun. It is more believable that they—as countless as they are—are suns that possess their own light, and that each individual one is also surrounded by its own planets and earthly inhabitants. And from this, the infinite wisdom and omnipotence of the Creator is to be perceived.

23.) Solar fire <note>likely referring to heat concentrated by burning lenses</note> produces far better effects in chemical works than the fire of a kitchen stove.

24.) No one doubts that man is endowed with perfect senses and with reason, and is capable of thought. But whether one can truly call the soul a "thinking being," the soul itself confesses that it does not know.
